3 Chapter 1 — Describing linear systems

3.1 1. Inputs, states, outputs and dimensions

  Key explanation

  Think of a linear system as a machine with an external signal u(t), internal memory
  x(t), and observed response y(t). The state collects enough information to determine
  future behaviour if the future input is known.

  Formula / calculation
  x'(t) = A x(t) + B u(t) y(t) = C x(t) + D u(t)


  Key explanation

  For n states, m inputs and p outputs: x is n×1, u is m×1, y is p×1; A is n×n, B is n×m,
  C is p×n, D is p×m. Always check dimensions before multiplying.

  Key explanation

  A describes internal dynamics; B shows how input changes the state; C converts state
  to output; D is the direct input-to-output feedthrough.


3.2 2. What linearity really means

  Formula / calculation
  L(a f + b g) = a L(f) + b L(g)


  Key explanation

  Both additivity and homogeneity are required. Differentiation, definite integration from
  0 to t, multiplication by a fixed function, evaluation at a fixed instant, and fixed matrix
  multiplication are linear operators on suitable function spaces.

  Key explanation

  A time-dependent coefficient does not automatically destroy linearity: y’ = a(t)y + b(t)u
  is linear. A product y² or uy is nonlinear. An affine constant offset, such as y’=ay+1
  without treating 1 as an input, does not describe a homogeneous linear map of state
  into derivative.




                                                   4
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                       Revision Guide


3.3 3. Block diagram language

  Key explanation

  Amplifier = multiply signal by a coefficient. Integrator = accumulate signal over time.
  Differentiator = derivative. Summing junction = add signed branches. Tap = copy a
  signal without splitting its value. An arrow indicates the flow of information.

  Key explanation

  An open-loop diagram contains no directed return path from downstream signals to
  earlier stages. A closed-loop system uses the output or state to modify what it re-
  ceives. Positive feedback can increase response; negative feedback commonly op-
  poses changes. Neither label alone proves stability.

  Formula / calculation
  Positive feedback: y = u + θy => y = u/(1-θ) if θ ≠ 1.


  Formula / calculation
  Negative feedback: y = G(u-Hy) => y = [G/(1+GH)]u.


  Exam method / worked solution
  EXAM METHOD: label every internal arrow, write an equation at each summing junction
  and block, eliminate internal variables, and simplify. Never decide the denominator
  sign without writing the summing-junction equation.


3.4 4. From differential equations to state-space

  Key explanation

  An nth-order scalar equation can be rewritten as n coupled first-order equations. The
  companion-matrix construction is the standard procedure.

  Formula / calculation
  y^(n) + a_(n-1)y^(n-1) + ... + a_1 y' + a_0 y = u


  Formula / calculation
  x = (y, y', ..., y^(n-1))^T; B=(0,...,0,1)^T


  Key explanation

  The companion matrix A has 1 on the superdiagonal, 0 in the remaining upper rows, and
  final row (-a_0,-a_1,...,-a_(n-1)). The output for the original variable is y=(1,0,...,0)x.

  Proof / reasoning

  The reason it works: the derivative of each state component is the next component,
  except the last derivative, which the original equation provides.




                                                  5
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                    Revision Guide


3.5 Worked example: damped oscillator

  Formula / calculation
  y'' + 3y' + 2y = u(t), x1=y, x2=y'


  Formula / calculation
  x1'=x2; x2'=-2x1-3x2+u


  Formula / calculation
  A=[[0,1],[-2,-3]], B=[[0],[1]], C=[1,0], D=0


  Key explanation

  The characteristic polynomial is λ²+3λ+2=(λ+1)(λ+2), so the unforced motion is a
  combination of exp(-t) and exp(-2t) and therefore decays. This is a quick physical
  sanity check.


3.6 Proof framework: reduction of order

  Revision checklist
  • State the differential equation and define x_j=y^(j-1).

  Revision checklist
  • Differentiate x_1,...,x_(n-1) to get x_j’=x_(j+1).

  Revision checklist
  • Rearrange the original equation for x_n’, retaining the input term.

  Revision checklist
  • Write the resulting equations as x’=Ax+Bu and confirm sizes. This completes the
  derivation.


4 Chapter 2 — Solving systems with matrix theory

4.1 1. Determinants, cofactors, adjugates

  Key explanation

  For a square matrix A, the determinant detects singularity: det A ≠ 0 exactly when A
  is invertible. Cofactor C_ij equals (-1)^(i+j) times the determinant after removing row
  i and column j. The adjugate is the transpose of the cofactor matrix.

  Formula / calculation
  A adj(A) = det(A) I; A^(-1)=adj(A)/det(A) when det A ≠ 0.




                                                  6
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                      Revision Guide



  Key explanation

  A triangular matrix has determinant equal to the product of its diagonal entries. Expand
  along a row or column with many zeros to minimise calculations.


4.2 2. Eigenvalues, characteristic polynomials and companion matrices

  Formula / calculation
  χ_A(λ) = det(λI-A) = λ^n - tr(A) λ^(n-1) + ... + (-1)^n det(A).


  Key explanation

  The course uses det(λI-A), not the alternate sign convention det(A-λI). Roots are eigen-
  values. To get an eigenvector v for λ solve (A-λI)v=0; never divide by a singular matrix.

  Key explanation

  Every monic polynomial λ^n+a_(n-1)λ^(n-1)+...+a_0 is the characteristic polynomial
  of its companion matrix. Hence one can turn a polynomial problem into a matrix prob-
  lem.


4.3 3. Norms and stability bounds

  Key explanation

  A vector norm measures the size of a vector. A compatible matrix operator norm
  measures its largest possible amplification of a vector: ||A||=sup_{||x||=1}||Ax||. Key
  inequalities include ||Ax||≤||A||||x|| and ||AB||≤||A||||B||.

  Key explanation

  Induced 1-norm is the maximum absolute column sum; induced infinity-norm is max-
  imum absolute row sum; Euclidean operator norm is the largest singular value. For
  complex vectors, use conjugate transpose in inner products.

  Key explanation

  A norm estimate can bound growth even if it does not determine the exact solution.
  Distinguish a bound from an equality.


4.4 4. Matrix exponentials and initial value problems

  Formula / calculation
  exp(At) = I + At + (At)^2/2! + (At)^3/3! + ...


  Formula / calculation
  x(t)=exp(At)x(0) + ∫_0^t exp(A(t-s)) B u(s) ds.




                                                    7
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                       Revision Guide



  Key explanation

  The first term is the free/homogeneous response determined by initial state; the inte-
  gral is the forced response. If A=PΛP^(-1), then exp(At)=P diag(exp(λ_i t)) P^(-1). If A
  has a Jordan block, polynomial factors in t can accompany exponentials.

  Key explanation

  Check a proposed solution by differentiating and substituting into x’=Ax+Bu and veri-
  fying x(0). For constant A, d(exp(At))/dt=A exp(At).


4.5 Worked example: solve a diagonal system

  Formula / calculation
  A=diag(-1,-2), B=(1,1)^T, u(t)=1, x(0)=(0,0)^T.


  Formula / calculation
  x1'=-x1+1 -> x1(t)=1-exp(-t).


  Formula / calculation
  x2'=-2x2+1 -> x2(t)=[1-exp(-2t)]/2.


  Key explanation

  At t=0 both components are zero; as t→∞ they tend to 1 and 1/2. The steady state is
  -A^(-1)B=(1,1/2)^T.


4.6 5. Outputs and transfer matrices

  Formula / calculation
  x'=Ax+Bu, y=Cx+Du, zero initial state.


  Formula / calculation
  G(s)=C(sI-A)^(-1)B+D.


  Key explanation

  G is p×m. For a single input/single output (SISO), it is a rational function. For multiple
  inputs/outputs (MIMO), every entry is a rational function. Transfer functions describe
  the zero-initial-condition input/output behaviour; they do not, by themselves, disclose
  all hidden internal modes.

  Key explanation

  A realization means finding A,B,C,D that generate a given G(s). Different state-space
  systems can produce the same transfer function. Check dimensions and calculate C(sI-
  A)^(-1)B+D explicitly.




                                                    8
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                     Revision Guide


4.7 Worked example: transfer function of an oscillator

  Formula / calculation
  A=[[0,1],[-2,-3]], B=[[0],[1]], C=[1,0], D=0.


  Formula / calculation
  sI-A=[[s,-1],[2,s+3]], determinant = s²+3s+2.


  Formula / calculation
  (sI-A)^(-1)B = [1,s]^T/(s²+3s+2).


  Formula / calculation
  G(s)=1/(s²+3s+2)=1/[(s+1)(s+2)].


  Key explanation

  Always test the result against the ODE: taking the Laplace transform of y”+3y’+2y=u
  under zero initial conditions yields the same G(s).



5 Chapter 3 — Laplace transforms

5.1 1. Definition and existence

  Formula / calculation
  L{f}(s)=F(s)=∫_0^∞ exp(-st) f(t) dt.


  Key explanation

  Think of the transform as a way to replace differentiation by multiplication by s, while
  retaining initial values as correction terms. The variable s is usually complex; conver-
  gence requires sufficient decay of exp(-st)f(t).

  Key explanation

  Typical transform pairs (for suitable Re(s)):   L{1}=1/s; L{exp(at)}=1/(s-a);
  L{t^n}=n!/s^(n+1); L{sin(ωt)}=ω/(s²+ω²); L{cos(ωt)}=s/(s²+ω²).


5.2 2. Essential operational rules

  Formula / calculation
  L{af+bg}=aF+bG.


  Formula / calculation
  L{f'}=sF-f(0); L{f''}=s²F-sf(0)-f'(0).


  Formula / calculation
  L{exp(at) f(t)}(s)=F(s-a).




                                                  9
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                      Revision Guide



  Formula / calculation
  L{t f(t)}(s) = -F'(s).


  Formula / calculation
  L{∫_0^t f(r)dr}=F(s)/s.


  Key explanation

  Convolution is (f*g)(t)=∫_0^t f(t-r)g(r)dr.    Under appropriate conditions
  L{f*g}=F(s)G(s). This explains why cascaded linear time-invariant systems mul-
  tiply their transfer functions.


5.3 Worked example: nonzero initial conditions

  Formula / calculation
  y''+3y'+2y=1, y(0)=1, y'(0)=0.


  Formula / calculation
  (s²Y-s)+(3sY-3)+2Y=1/s.


  Formula / calculation
  Y(s)=[1/s+s+3]/[(s+1)(s+2)].


  Key explanation

  For the easiest time-domain solution, find the constant particular solution y_p=1/2.
  The homogeneous solution is c1 exp(-t)+c2 exp(-2t). Using y(0)=1 and y’(0)=0 gives
  c1=1, c2=-1/2.

  Formula / calculation
  y(t)=1/2 + exp(-t) - (1/2)exp(-2t).


  Key explanation

  Verify at t=0 and check the differential equation. In exams, forgetting the initial-value
  terms is a common major error.


5.4 3. Partial fractions and inverse transforms

  Key explanation

  Factor the denominator. For a simple real pole at -a, A/(s+a) becomes A exp(-at). Re-
  peated poles require A/(s+a)+B/(s+a)^2+...; the power k corresponds to t^(k-1)exp(-
  at)/(k-1)!.

  Formula / calculation
  1/[(s+1)(s+2)]=1/(s+1)-1/(s+2).




                                             10
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                     Revision Guide



  Formula / calculation
  L^(-1){1/[(s+1)(s+2)]}=exp(-t)-exp(-2t).


  Key explanation

  For irreducible quadratic factors, complete the square and compare with sine/cosine
  formulas. For improper rational functions, first use polynomial division before partial
  fractions.


5.5 4. Laplace transform of state-space models

  Formula / calculation
  sX(s)-x(0)=AX(s)+BU(s).


  Formula / calculation
  X(s)=(sI-A)^(-1)x(0)+(sI-A)^(-1)BU(s).


  Formula / calculation
  Y(s)=C(sI-A)^(-1)x(0)+G(s)U(s).


  Key explanation

  The resolvent (sI-A)^(-1) connects the time-domain exponential and Laplace-domain
  algebra. Always separate the initial-state response from the input response.



6 Chapter 4 — Transfer functions and stability

6.1 1. Poles, zeros and minimal interpretation

  Key explanation

  The poles of a reduced scalar rational transfer function are roots of its denominator;
  its zeros are roots of the numerator. A transfer function may show cancellations: they
  can conceal internal state dynamics, so distinguish input/output stability from internal
  stability.

  Key explanation

  For a continuous-time finite-dimensional autonomous system x’=Ax, asymptotic sta-
  bility holds when every eigenvalue of A has strictly negative real part. Eigenvalues
  with positive real part lead to growth. Imaginary-axis eigenvalues require more care;
  Jordan structure matters.


6.2 2. Frequency response

  Formula / calculation
  If u(t)=exp(iωt), the steady-state output is related to G(iω).




                                                 11
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                       Revision Guide



  Key explanation

  Magnitude |G(iω)| describes gain, while arg G(iω) describes phase shift. For real sine-
  wave inputs one takes the appropriate real/imaginary part. The Fourier/frequency-
  response viewpoint helps interpret how a system treats slow and fast oscillations.

  Key explanation

  Bode magnitude plots typically use 20 log10|G(iω)| in dB against logarithmic fre-
  quency; Bode phase plots show arg G(iω). A Nyquist plot traces G(iω) in the complex
  plane as frequency varies. The full Nyquist stability criterion needs its hypotheses and
  contour treatment; do not simply count crossings without defining the curve

  Key explanation

  and open-loop poles.


6.3 Worked example: frequency response of a first-order filter

  Formula / calculation
  G(s)=1/(s+2). G(iω)=1/(2+iω)=(2-iω)/(4+ω²).


  Formula / calculation
  |G(iω)|=1/sqrt(4+ω²), phase=-arctan(ω/2).


  Key explanation

  At ω=0, gain is 1/2; at large ω, gain tends to zero. The system suppresses high-
  frequency components. Its pole -2 lies in the left half-plane, so this first-order realiza-
  tion is asymptotically stable.


6.4 3. Stability workflow for exam questions

  Revision checklist
  • Identify the question: internal stability of A, BIBO stability of a transfer function, or
  stability of a closed feedback loop.

  Revision checklist
  • For internal stability compute the eigenvalues of the state matrix. For rational BIBO
  input/output stability inspect the poles after cancellation, subject to the stated assump-
  tions.

  Revision checklist
  • For feedback derive the correct closed-loop denominator, often 1+G(s)H(s) for neg-
  ative feedback.

  Revision checklist
  • Check the roots of the relevant characteristic polynomial. If using a frequency plot,
  state and apply the appropriate Nyquist assumptions.



                                                12
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                      Revision Guide



  Revision checklist
  • Mention hidden unstable cancellations if the realisation is not known to be minimal.


7 Chapter 5 — Feedback control

7.1 1. Algebra of closed-loop systems

  Formula / calculation
  e=r-Hy, u=K e, y=P u.


  Formula / calculation
  y=P K(r-Hy) -> (1+PKH)y=PKr.


  Formula / calculation
  T(s)=y/r=PK/(1+PKH).


  Key explanation

  Here P is the plant, K is the controller, H is the feedback/sensor element, and r is the
  reference. This algebra is valid for a scalar loop; in MIMO systems multiplication order
  matters and inverses are matrices.


7.2 Worked example: choose a proportional controller

  Formula / calculation
  Plant P(s)=1/(s+2), unity negative feedback H(s)=1, K(s)=k.


  Formula / calculation
  T(s)=k/(s+2+k).


  Key explanation

  The closed-loop pole is -(2+k): for k>-2 it is strictly negative. Increasing positive k
  moves it left and speeds up the ideal first-order response, but the real-world trade-offs
  (noise, limits, uncertainty) are not captured by this elementary example.

  Key explanation

  For a unit step, the steady-state output is k/(k+2) when stable, so proportional control
  alone leaves a steady-state tracking error of 2/(k+2).


7.3 2. Polynomial algebra and controller design

  Key explanation

  The lecture sequence includes rational functions, greatest common divisors, Euclidean
  algorithms, matrix completion, and stabilising controller parameterisations. These
  techniques organise feedback design



                                                 13
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                    Revision Guide



  Key explanation

  algebraically rather than relying exclusively on diagrams.

  Key explanation

  To use the Euclidean algorithm on polynomials, divide P by Q to get P=SQ+R, where
  deg R<deg Q; replace (P,Q) by (Q,R) until remainder zero. The final nonzero remainder
  (normalised monic) is gcd(P,Q). Back-substitution yields Bézout coefficients X,Y with
  XP+YQ=gcd(P,Q).

  Key explanation

  If gcd(P,Q)=1, a Bézout identity XP+YQ=1 proves coprimeness and can help solve
  stabilisation equations. In all controller problems, distinguish algebraic cancellation
  from physically acceptable/internal stability.


7.4 3. Lyapunov stability

  Key explanation

  For x’=Ax, choose a real symmetric positive-definite matrix P and quadratic energy
  V(x)=x^T P x. Then V’=x^T(A^T P+PA)x.

  Formula / calculation
  A^T P + P A = -Q, Q positive definite.


  Key explanation

  If P is also positive definite, then V>0 away from the origin and V’=-x^TQx<0 away
  from the origin, establishing asymptotic stability. For complex matrices use conjugate
  transpose A* and x*.

  Key explanation

  For continuous-time Hurwitz A and a given Q>0, the Lyapunov equation has a unique
  positive-definite solution P. The lecture slides also discuss MATLAB calculations and
  related matrix equations.


7.5 Worked Lyapunov example

  Formula / calculation
  A=diag(-1,-2), Q=I. Seek diagonal P=diag(p1,p2).


  Formula / calculation
  A^T P+PA=diag(-2p1,-4p2)=-I.


  Formula / calculation
  p1=1/2, p2=1/4; both positive.




                                                 14
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                      Revision Guide



  Formula / calculation
  V(x)=(1/2)x1²+(1/4)x2² and V'=-(x1²+x2²).


  Key explanation

  This proves asymptotic decay without explicitly solving the system.


7.6 4. MATLAB checks

  Key explanation

  Common checks: eig(A) for eigenvalues; det(A) for determinant; expm(A*t) for ma-
  trix exponential; inv(s*eye(n)-A) when using symbolic variables; ss(A,B,C,D) and tf(...)
  when relevant toolboxes are available; bode, nyquist and step for system plots;
  lyap(A,Q) for the continuous Lyapunov equation A P+P A^T=-Q (note the exact trans-
  pose convention in each function).

  Key explanation

  Use MATLAB to verify reasoning, not replace it: your written answer should state the
  mathematical criterion, show enough algebra, and interpret the result.

9 Self-test: 12 exam-style questions

9.1 1. Give dimensions of A,B,C,D when x∈R^4, u∈R^2, y∈R^3.

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: A 4×4, B 4×2, C 3×4, D 3×2.


9.2 2. Is Lf(t)=t f(t) linear? Is Lf(t)=f(t)^2 linear?

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: The first is linear for fixed t-multiplier; the square is nonlinear.


9.3 3. Convert y”+4y’+5y=u into companion state-space.

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: x=(y,y’)^T; A=[[0,1],[-5,-4]], B=(0,1)^T, C=(1,0), D=0.


9.4 4. Find eigenvalues of A=diag(-2,3). Is x’=Ax stable?

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: -2 and 3. Unstable because one eigenvalue has positive real part.


9.5 5. Compute exp(At) for A=diag(-1,-3).

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: diag(exp(-t),exp(-3t)).


9.6 6. Find G(s) for y’+5y=2u and y(0)=0.

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: G(s)=2/(s+5).




                                               16
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                       Revision Guide


9.7 7. Find L{t exp(-2t)}.

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: 1/(s+2)^2.


9.8 8. Find inverse Laplace of 1/[(s+2)(s+3)].

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: exp(-2t)-exp(-3t).


9.9 9. Derive closed-loop negative-feedback transfer for P and H.

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: P/(1+PH), with the controller incorporated into P if appropriate.


9.10 10. Find closed-loop pole for P(s)=1/(s+4), unity feedback K=k.

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: Pole = -(4+k), stable for k>-4.


9.11 11. Find the magnitude of G(iω) if G(s)=3/(s+1).

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: 3/sqrt(1+ω²).


9.12 12. Solve A^TP+PA=-I with A=-2 (scalar).

  Exam method / worked solution
  Solution: P=1/4>0; V=x²/4 decreases as x’=-2x.



10 Common marking pitfalls

  Revision checklist
  • Not stating zero-initial-state assumption when deriving a transfer function.

  Revision checklist
  • Writing χ_A as det(A-λI) without noting the sign convention.

  Revision checklist
  • Using incorrect companion-matrix coefficient order or signs.




                                              17
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                      Revision Guide



  Revision checklist
  • Ignoring input/output/matrix dimensions, especially in MIMO products.

  Revision checklist
  • Confusing eigenvalues of A with poles of a reduced, possibly nonminimal transfer
  function.

  Revision checklist
  • Dropping y(0) and y’(0) in Laplace transformations.

  Revision checklist
  • Treating positive/negative feedback as automatically stable/unstable.

  Revision checklist
  • Using a Lyapunov equation without verifying positive definiteness.

  Revision checklist
  • Reporting numerical software outputs without explaining the mathematical principle.

  Chapter 5.

  Frame 6: 6 Chapter 1: Linear systems and their description
  What the slide says: In linear systems, we consider a machine made up of several components, which are connected together.
  We take t > 0 to be time and consider the evolution of the system through time. The machine has an input u = u(t), and output y =
  y(t) and the internal state of the machine is described by a state x = x(t), and we take u, x, y to be vector-valued functions of t.
  The state of a system is a set of variables whose values, together with the input and the equations describing the dynamics, will
  describe the future state and output of the system. Usually, we want to know how y(t) depends upon u(t). The component parts of
  the machine are represented by various linear operators. We consider a special class of linear systems that we can analyze by
  means of linear algebra. We obtain: • general results which enable us to classify and describe a significant class of linear
  systems, called (A, B, C, D); • specific methods for solving these linear systems; • results that are in a form that allows effective
  and explicit computation of solutions, usually using computers.

  How to study it: System checkpoint: locate u, x, y and any A,B,C,D matrices. Confirm dimensions, distinguish initial-condition effects
  from input effects, and calculate a small special case.




                                                                   20
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                               Revision Guide




  Frame 7: 7 Linear systems and their description
  What the slide says: Let C be the field of complex numbers, let V and W be vector spaces over C, so λf + µg ∈V for all f , g ∈V
  and λ, µ ∈C. (We use C since the results from MATH220 about square matrices work best for C, and we also use some results
  from MATH215 complex analysis.) Time is t > 0. A map L : V →W is called linear if L(λf + µg) = λLf + µLg. The following give the
  basic examples with V = W = {continuously differentiable functions f : [0, ∞) →C}. i) Differentiation Lf = df dt ; symbolised by [d/dt];
  ii) Integration Lf (x) = ∫■x 0 f (t) dt, symbolised as [ ∫■ ]; iii) an amplifier is multiplication by a ∈C, symbolised as [a]; iv)
  Multiplication by h ∈V , Lf (t) = h(t)f (t) symbolised as [h]; v) Evaluation at t0, f ■→f (t0) symbolised as [δt0].

  How to study it: System checkpoint: locate u, x, y and any A,B,C,D matrices. Confirm dimensions, distinguish initial-condition effects
  from input effects, and calculate a small special case.


  Frame 8: 8 Summing junctions and taps
  What the slide says: + u1 u2 u1 + u2 Diagram of a summing junction, to add signals . u u u Diagram of tap. Think of a tap as a
  point on an electrical circuit where wires are connected, so that the voltage is the same at all wires making the connection. Unlike
  a water tap or junction in a plumbing system, the tap does not divide the input into different outputs.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 9: Kirchoff’s laws
  What the slide says: Kirchhoff’s law on currents: the algebraic sum of currents leaving a junction or node equals the algebraic
  sum of currents entering that node. (This is like a summing junction in a block diagram.) Kirchhoff’s law on voltages: the algebraic
  sum of all voltages around a closed path in a circuit is zero. (This is like the taps in a block diagram satisfy.)

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 10: 9 Amplifiers, integrators and differentiators
  What the slide says: Diagram of amplifier a u au The effect of the amplifier is to multiply by some a ∈C. The term amplifier is
  used here even when 0 < a < 1, so the amplitude of the signal is decreased. Diagram of integrator ∫■ u ∫■ u Diagram of
  differentiator d/dt u ...

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 11: 10 Producing block diagrams
  What the slide says: You can produce block diagrams either by: (i) drawing with pen on paper, and photographing with mobile
  telephone; (ii) drawing on whiteboard, and photographing with mobile telephone; (iii) using tikz pictures in Latex (this is hard
  work); (iv) drawing on tablets.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 12: 11 Block Diagrams
  What the slide says: Let V be the space of infinitely differentiable functions f : (0, ∞) →C. Let u ∈V be the input, y ∈V be the
  output. A diagram is a graph built up from vertices u, y and others chosen from {■ u, y, δ0, ⊕, ·, d/dt, ∫■ , a, h }■ which are
  connected by directed edges, drawn as arrows. (1) u is the input and y is the output. The vertices u and y have degree one;
  whereas all other vertices have degree two or three with one or two arrows pointing into the vertex, and one or two pointing out.
  (2) The graph is simple, so there are no loops based at vertices or multiple edges between vertices. All vertices lie on some
  directed path of consecutive arrows from u to y. (3) If the diagram contains a circuit, then we say that there is feedback. (4) If the
  diagram does not contain a circuit, then the system is ‘open loop’ or ‘straight through’.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 13: 12 Open loop system
  What the slide says: We can build up more complicated systems as in y = 3 ∫■ u + 2u′ + 6u. . . 6 + 2 d/dt + 3 ∫■ u u 6u u u 2u
  2du/dt u u 3u 3 ∫■ u y




                                                                   21
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                                   Revision Guide




  How to study it: System checkpoint: locate u, x, y and any A,B,C,D matrices. Confirm dimensions, distinguish initial-condition effects
  from input effects, and calculate a small special case.


  Frame 14: 13 Feedback
  What the slide says: [See video: V1 feedback block diagram] Open loop Here the input u is subject to linear operations and
  produces an output y. For example y(t) = atu(t) + u′′(t). Some machines are open loop, for instance rockets, or primitive turbines.
  Closed loop or feedback systems Here we take the input, subject it to linear operations, and also take the output, feed it back into
  the system, apply linear operations to the output and then add the modified input and the modified output. Most modern
  appliances involve some feedback or control systems. For instance, car engines, wind turbines involve feedback systems to
  control their rate of rotation.

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.


  Frame 15: 13 Positive feedback
  What the slide says: Example 1. (Bird populations) Let x be the number of birds on an isolated island, say puffins on Ailsa
  Craig. The birth rate of birds is proportional to the number of birds, and birds can come and go by flying to other nesting sites at
  rate u, so, for some k > 0, dx dt = kx + u. Example 2. (Turbocharger) A car engine has a turbocharger. The faster the engine
  revolves, the more exhaust it produces, so the turbine forces more air into the engine, so the engine goes faster and so on. Most
  practical devices incorporate some negative feedback so that they do not damage themselves or their users.

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.


  Frame 16: 15 Black’s amplifier
  What the slide says: + . θ u u + θy y θy y Example 3. (Black’s amplifier) Let 0 < θ < 1. The output y is fed back into the input u,
  after multiplication by θ, so y = θy + u, hence y = u/(1 −θ) is an amplified version of the input. This will also amplify any noise, so is
  not practical by itself as an amplifier. In this feedback system, the summing junction is beside the input, whereas the tap is beside
  the output.

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 17: 16 Negative Feedback via air resistance
  What the slide says: Example. Feedback can also occur by force of nature. A cyclist pedals harder, hence goes faster, but the
  faster the cyclist goes, the greater the air resistance. Let m be the mass of the cyclist, v the velocity, k a constant of
  proportionality, and u the force imparted on the pedals. Then by Newton’s second law of motion, mdv dt = −kv + u which we can
  express as a feedback system v = −k m ∫■ v + 1 m ∫■ u, and the sign of −k/m indicates negative feedback.

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.


  Frame 18: 17 Negative feedback diagram
  What the slide says: + ∫■ 1/m . −k u v −kv v

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.


  Frame 19: 18 Matrix amplifier
  What the slide says: Diagram of matrix amplifier B U BU The number of columns of the matrix B equals the number of rows of
  U, so that BU is well defined. We can use summing junctions, taps, differentiators and integrators with vector inputs. So we can
  form block diagrams with vector inputs. In this way, we can deal with multiple inputs and multiple outputs.

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 20: 19 Linear differential operators
  What the slide says: Let t be the independent variable. By combining amplifiers, summing junctions and differentiators, we can
  construct linear differential operators Lf (t) = an(t)dnf dtn + an−1(t)dn−1f dtn−1 + · · · + a0(t)f (t); the number of derivatives n is the




                                                                      22
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                                   Revision Guide




  order of L; the aj(t) are the coefficient (functions). When the aj are constants, we talk about a linear differential operator with
  constant coefficients. A linear equation of order n is an(t)dnf dtn + an−1(t)dn−1f dtn−1 + · · · + a0(t)f (t) = u(t), where an(t), . . . ,
  a0(t), u(t) are given and f (t) is to be found.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 21: 20 Differential equations as feedback systems
  What the slide says: Proposition The differential equation any(n)(t) + · · · + a0y = bmu(m) + bm−1u(m−1)(t) + · · · + b0u(t) with
  constant coefficients can be realized as a feedback system with input u and output y involving taps, amplifiers, summing junction,
  integrators and differentiations. Proof. When an ■= 0, we integrate n times and divide by an to get y = −an−1 an ∫■ y −· · · −a0 an
  ∫■(n) y + bm an ∫■(n) u(m) + · · · + b0 an ∫■(n) u. Then it is straightforward to realize the system by introducing an intermediate
  state x via x = Bu, ...

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.

  Frame 25: 23 Damped harmonic oscillator
  What the slide says: Damped harmonic oscillators are important, because they (i) arise in various physical and electrical
  systems; (ii) exhibit the effects that describe more general linear systems. So this example will reappear. Let t be time and y
  displacement from rest of a mass on a spring. The velocity is y′ and acceleration y′′, the mass is driven by an external driving
  force u(t). The equation is y′′ + βy′ + γy = u where β and γ are constants; usually γ > 0 and β ≥0. This models the suspension of a
  car moving along a road. Here u(t) represents the force imparted on the car by the road; the suspension involves a spring which
  gives a restoring force −γy, while the shock absorbers give a damping force −βy′. We can write this as a feedback system, using
  the formula y = −β ∫■ y −γ ∫■∫■ y + ∫■∫■ u.

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 26: 24 Harmonic oscillator as a feedback system
  What the slide says: ∫■ + ∫■ · · −β + −γ ∫■ u y

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 27: 25 Matrix form of the damped harmonic oscillator
  What the slide says: We often choose input u(t) = eiωt = cos(ωt) + i sin(ωt), with angular frequency ω. We introduce a new state
  variable v, the velocity, so y′ = v. Then we have a pair of equations y′ = v and v′ = −βv −γy + u. We put these together in a matrix
  equation X = [■y v ]■ , B = [■0 1 ]■ A = [■0 1 −γ −β ]■ so the matrix form of the differential equation is dX dt = AX + Bu.

  Frame 28: 26 Reduction of order
  What the slide says: [see video: V3 reduction of order] Lemma The linear differential equation dny dtn + an−1(t)dn−1y dtn−1 + ·
  · · + a0(t)y(t) = u(t), may be expressed as the matrix system dX dt = AX + Bu where X is (n × 1), A is (n × n) and B is (n × 1). Thus
  we replace a nth order differential equation with one independent variable with a first order differential equation with a (n × 1)
  vector independent variable. ...

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 29: 27 Proof of reduction of order

  ... ... ... −a0 −a1 . . . −an−1 ■ ■■■■■ so the differential equation is dX dt = AX + Bu.

  Frame 30: 28 Constant coefficient case
  What the slide says: The advantages are that first order differential equations are easier to solve than nth order equation, and in
  this case, we can use linear algebra on the matrix A; see the discussion on frame 52 below. If the aj are constant, or equivalently
  A is constant, then the system is said to have constant coefficients, and the differential equation can be expressed as the
  feedback system X = A ∫■ X + B ∫■ U.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.

  BU) X AX X

  . . 0 0 1 ... ... ... ... ... −a0 −a1 . . . −an−1 ■ ■■■■■ is called a companion matrix (as in frame 27; note the order and signs of the

  Frame 33: 31 Chapter 2: Solving linear systems by matrix theory
  What the slide says: Determinants To obtain the determinant of A = ■ ■ a11 a12 a13 a21 a22 a23 a31 a32 a33 ■ ■ recall the
  chess-board of signs ■ ■ + − + − + − + − + ■ ■, pick one column, say the second, then multiply out with sign from above det A =
  −a12 ■■■■ a21 a23 a31 a33 ■■■■+ a22 ■■■■ a11 a13 a31 a33 ■■■■−a32 ■■■■ a11 a13 a21 a23 ■■■■ where the 2 ×
  2 determinants exclude the row and column of their coefficient.

  Frame 34: 32 Cofactors
  What the slide says: Definition (Adjugate) The determinant of the submatrix of A that excludes row j and column k, multiplied by
  (−1)j+k, is called the cofactor Ajk of ajk, so det A = n ∑■ j=1 ajkAjk is the expansion by column k for all k = 1, . . . , n. The
  adjugate matrix adj(A) is the transpose of the matrix [Ajk] of cofactors. Proposition (i) For all square matrices A adj(A) = (det A)I.
  (ii) A square matrix is said to be lower triangular if all the entries above the leading diagonal are zero. The determinant of a lower
  triangular matrix equals the product of the entries on the leading diagonal.




                                                                   24
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                                 Revision Guide




  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 35: 33 Characteristic polynomial
  What the slide says: Definition (Characteristic polynomial) The characteristic polynomial of a (n × n) complex matrix A is χA(λ) =
  det(λI −A), where I is the (n × n) identity matrix. In MATH220, the lectures used cA(λ) = det(A −λI). The definition used in the
  MATH6342 lectures is standard in control theory. Also χA(λ) = det(λI −A) = λn −λn−1 trace (A) + · · · + (−1)n det A, so χA(λ) is a
  monic polynomial of degree n. Lemma If det(sI −A) ■= 0, then sI −A is invertible and (sI −A)−1 = (det(sI −A))−1adj(sI −A). This
  may or may not be an appropriate formula for computing ...

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 36: 34 All monic polynomials are characteristic polynomials

  Frame 39: 37 Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
  What the slide says: ■z + u, w■= ■z, w■+ ■u, w■ ■λz, w■= λ■z, w■, ■z, w■= ■w, z■ On V the standard norm is the
  Euclidean norm for z = column(zj)n j=1 ■(zj)n j=1■= (■n ∑■ j=1 |zj|2)■1/2 = (¯z■z)1/2 = ■z, z■1/2. Proposition
  (Cauchy–Schwarz inequality) All z, w ∈V satisfy (i) |■z, w■| ≤■z■■w■, (ii) ■z + w■≤■z■+ ■w■.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and

  Frame 40: 38 Proof of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
  What the slide says: Proof. (i) Recall that a nonzero complex number ζ has a polar decomposition ζ = seiθ, where θ ∈(−π, π]
  and s = |ζ| > 0; so e−iθζ = s > 0. If ■z, w■= 0, then (i) is clearly true. Otherwise, there exists u ∈C such that u¯u = 1 and u■z,
  w■= |■z, w■|. Now we have a real quadratic in t which is non negative 0 ≤■tw + uz■2 = ■tw + uz, tw + uz■ = t2■w, w■+ t■w,
  uz■+ ■uz, tw■+ ■uz, uz■ = t2■w■2 + 2t|■z, w■| + ■z■2. We cannot have a pair of distinct real roots, since y = t2■w■2 +
  2t|■z, w■| + ■z■2 is a parabola that does not cross the t-axis. Hence this quadratic has discriminant b2 −4ac ≤0, so 4|■z, w■|2
  ≤4■z■2■w■2.

  What the slide says: (ii) By (i) we have ■z + w■2 = ■z + w, z + w■ = ■z, z■+ ■z, w■+ ■w, z■+ ■w, w■ = ■z■2 + 2ℜ■z, w■+
  ■w■2 ≤■z■2 + 2■z■■w■+ ■w■2 = (■ ■z■+ ■w■ )■2. Suppose that A and B are (n × n) complex matrices, and that V =

  Frame 42: 40 Adjoint of a matrix
  What the slide says: Definition (i) The transpose of a n × m matrix A = [ajk] is the m × n matrix A■= [akj], obtained by
  interchanging rows with columns. (ii) We define the adjoint of a n × m matrix A = [ajk] to be the m × n matrix A′ = [¯akj] given
  interchanging the rows and columns and taking the complex conjugate. If A is real, then A′ = A■, the transpose. We have ■Ax,
  y■= ■x, A′y■. This is MATLAB-style notation commonly used in linear systems engineering; in linear algebra the notation A∗is
  used for adjoint, and in physics A† is used. The adjoint is often called the Hermitian conjugate. Definition The norm of a (n × n)
  matrix is ■A■= sup{■Av■: v ∈V ; ■v■≤1}.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.


  Frame 43: 41 Estimates on the norm of a matrix
  What the slide says: Proposition (i) For a square diagonal matrix ∆with diagonal entries λ1, . . . , λn, ■∆■= max{|λj| : j = 1, . . . ,
  n}. (ii) Let A have columns A = [A1, A2, . . . , An], and let column Aj ∈Cn×1 have norm ■Aj■. Then ■A■≤(∑■n j=1 ■Aj■2)1/2.
  Proof. (i) Let (ej)n j=1 be the usual orthonormal basis of Cn×1 and v = ∑■n j=1 vjej, so ■v■= (∑■n j=1 |vj|2)1/2. Then ∆v = ∑■n
  j=1 vj∆ej = ∑■n j=1 vjλjej, so ■∆v■2 = n ∑■ j=1 |λjvj|2 ≤max k |λk|2 n ∑■ j=1 |vj|2 = max k |λk|2■v■2. We can achieve equality
  in this inequality by considering the largest |λk|, and selecting v = ek.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.


  Frame 44: 42 Proof of matrix norm estimates
  What the slide says: (ii) Here we have Av = ∑■n j=1 vjAej = ∑■n j=1 vjAj, so by the triangle inequality, ■Av■≤ n ∑■ j=1
  |vj|■Aj■≤ (■n ∑■ j=1 |vj|2)■1/2(■n ∑■ j=1 ■Aj■2)■1/2 , where the last step follows by Cauchy–Schwarz, so ■Av■≤ (■n ∑■
  j=1 ■Aj■2)■1/2 ■v■. This inequality (ii) is usually an overestimate, but it does show that the norm of any finite matrix is finite.

  Frame 45: 43 Properties of the norm of a matrix
  What the slide says: Lemma The matrix norm satisfies ■A + B■≤■A■+ ■B■; ■λA■= |λ|■A■; ■AB■≤■A■■B■. Proof. Let v
  ∈V have ■v■≤1. We have ■(A + B)v■= ■Av + Bv■≤■Av■+ ■Bv■≤■A■+ ■B■ so ■A + B■≤■A■+ ■B■. We have ■(λA)v■=
  |λ|■Av■, so ■λA■= |λ|■A■. We observe that ■A■= inf{t : ■Aw■≤t■w■: ∀w ∈V }. Then with w = Bv, we have
  ■(AB)v■≤■A■■■Bv■≤■A■■B■■v■, so ■AB■≤■A■■B■.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.


  Frame 46: 44 Rank of a matrix
  What the slide says: Lemma For X1, . . . , Xn ∈Cm×1, the following quantities are equal: (i) the dimension of the vector space V
  spanned by X1, . . . , Xn; (ii) the number of linearly independent columns in the m × n matrix A = [X1 . . . Xn]; (iii) the number of
  linearly independent rows in A; (iv) the number of nonzero rows in any equivalent echelon form of A. For a proof, see MATH220
  Theorem 3.24. These can be computed in MATLAB and Scilab using rank(A).

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.

  identity matrix. We can form A, A2, A3, . . . by matrix multiplication, and hence given g(z) = amzm + · · · + a0 we build polynomials

  Frame 48: 46 Characteristic equation
  What the slide says: The eigenvalue equation is Av = λv where v ■= 0 is the eigenvector and λ ∈C the eigenvalue. Lemma The
  eigenvalues of n × n complex matrix A are the roots of the characteristic equation χA(λ) = 0. Proof. Recall that det(sI −A) = χA(s).
  By the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, there are n complex roots, counted according to algebraic multiplicity. When χA(λ) = 0,
  the matrix λI −A is not invertible, so there exists v ∈V , with v ■= 0 and Av = λv and λ is an eigenvalue. Conversely, if there exists
  v ■= 0 and λ ∈C such that Av = λv, then λI −A is not invertible, so χA(λ) = 0.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 49: 47 Similar to a diagonal matrix
  What the slide says: (The geometric multiplicity of λ is the number of linearly independent solutions of Av = λv. Now by a slight
  extension of this Lemma, one can show that 1 ≤(geometric multiplicity) ≤(algebraic multiplicity).) Lemma For a complex n × n
  matrix A, the following are equivalent: (i) There exists an invertible matrix S such that ∆= S−1AS is a diagonal matrix; (ii) there
  exist n linearly independent eigenvectors of A; (iii) there exists a basis of Cn that consists of eigenvectors of A.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.

  Frame 53: 51 Cayley–Hamilton Theorem
  What the slide says: Theorem Let A be a n × n complex matrix with characteristic polynomial χA(s). Then χA(A) = 0. Proof See
  project topics. Complex exponential For z ∈C write z = ℜz + iℑz where ℜz is the real part and ℑz is the imaginary part. We define
  exp(z) = ez = 1 + z + z2 2! + z3 3! + . . . , which converges for all z ∈C. We have exp(z + w) = exp(z) exp(w) and (d/dz) exp(z) =
  exp(z). Also, eiθ = cos θ + i sin θ has |eiθ| = 1. Hence ez has modulus |ez| = eℜz and argument arg ez = ℑz. In engineering, this
  is often called the phase.

  How to study it: Proof checkpoint: identify the assumptions, the statement to establish, and the exact justification for each algebraic
  or analytic step. Reproduce the proof without referring to the slide.

  Frame 54: 52 Matrix exponential exp(A) or expm (A)
  What the slide says: [see video: V19 calculating matrix exponentials] For any n × n complex matrix A, we define the matrix
  exponential by exp(A) = I + A + A2/2! + · · · + Am/m! + . . . . Proposition (Wedderburn) (i) For any n × n complex matrix A, the
  exponential series converges, and ■exp(A)■≤e■A■. (ii) exp(zA) exp(wA) = exp((z + w)A) for all z, w ∈C; (iii) exp(zA) has inverse
  exp(−zA) for all z ∈C; (iv) Let λ be an eigenvalue of A. Then ezλ is an eigenvalue of exp(zA). (v) d dz exp(zA) = A exp(zA).

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.

  Frame 58: 56 Exponential of a diagonable matrix

  S such that exp(tA) = S ■ ■■■■■ etλ1 0 0 . . . 0 etλ2 0 ... ... ... ... ... 0 . . . 0 etλn ■ ■■■■■ S−1. Proof. Introduce the matrix S =

  Frame 59: 57 Exponentials of diagonable matrices
  What the slide says: Proposition Suppose that A has distinct eigenvalues λj such that ℜλj ≤κ for all j = 1, . . . , n, all the
  eigenvalues lie in the closed left half plane {λ : ℜλ ≤κ} which consists of the points in the complex plane that lie on or to the left of
  the vertical line {λ : ℜλ = κ}. (i) Then the general solution of dX dt = AX is X = ∑■n j=1 ajeλjtXj, where Xj is an eigenvector
  corresponding to λj and aj ∈C are arbitrary. (ii) There exists M such that ■exp(tA)■≤Meκt (t ≥0). (iii) In particular, suppose that
  ℜλj ≤0 for all j = 1, . . . , n. Then there exists M such that ■exp(tA)■≤M (t ≥0).

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.

  Frame 62: 60 SISO (A,B,C,D)
  What the slide says: [see video V6 (A,B,C,D) linear systems] Let A, B, C, D be constant complex matrices with shapes: A (n ×
  n); B (n × 1); C (1 × n); D (1 × 1). Then the linear system with one input, n states and one output is dX dt = AX + Bu y = CX + Du
  Here t is time, u is the input, X is the state, and y is the output. We call the system single-input single-output or SISO. Examples.
  (i) An electrical fan is a SISO system. The input is electricity, and the output is moving air. The states can involve the speed of
  rotation, voltage, current and so on. (ii) A wind turbine has input moving air and output electricity.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 63: 61 MIMO (A,B,C,D) (see diagram on 84)
  What the slide says: Definition (A,B,C,D) Let A, B, C, D be constant complex matrices with shapes: A (n × n); B (n × k); C (m ×
  n); D (m × k). Then the continuous time linear system with k inputs, n states and m outputs is dX dt = AX + BU, Y = CX + DU.
  Here U ∈Ck×1 is the input, X ∈Cn×1 is the state, and Y ∈Cm×1 is the output, which all depend upon t. The matrices are: A=state
  matrix (main transformation); B= input matrix; C= output matrix; D=straight through matrix (see diagram on frame 84)

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 64: 62 MIMO (A,B,C,D)
  What the slide says: This is the standard form for continuous time linear systems, as used in MATLAB etc. i) If k = 1, then we
  say the system is single input; if m = 1, then the system is single output. If k = m = 1, then the system is SISO. ii) If k > 1, then we
  say the system is multi input; if m > 1 then the system is multi output. iii) If k ≥1 and m ≥1, then we call the system MIMO. (SISO
  is a special case of MIMO) We write MIMO as a rectangular (n + k) × (n + m) block array with sizes [■A B C D ]■ [■n × n n × k m
  × n m × k ]■

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 65: 63 Domestic MIMO
  What the slide says: Example 1. A washing machine is a MIMO system. The inputs are cold water, soap powder, and electricity;
  whereas the outputs are hot soapy water, cold rinsing water and hot air. The state of the washing machine can be complicated,
  and relate to the rotation of the drum, temperature of various components, washing cycles and so on. Example 2. A domestic
  central heating system is a MIMO system with inputs gas, cold air, electricity and cold water; whereas the outputs are hot air and
  hot water. Example 3. Mobile telephone networks are MIMO systems. There are multiple transmitting antennas, and multiple
  receiving antennas. Massive MIMO is used to model 5G wireless transmission. In a given district, the may be 1000 receivers,
  and 100 transmitters; so we need a matrix A of size 1000 × 1000. This explains the terminology ‘massive’.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 66: 64 MIMO transposed
  What the slide says: Let A, B, C, D be complex matrices with shapes A (n × n); B (n × k); C (m × n); D (m × k) Recall (m × n) ×
  (n × n) × (n × k) = (m × k) Then (A■, C■, B■, D■) also gives a linear system [■A■ C■ B■ D■ ]■ [■n × n n × m k × n k × m ]■
  where det(sI −A■) = det(sI −A).

  Frame 67: 65 Solving the basic linear differential equation
  What the slide says: Theorem Suppose that A is a constant (n × n) matrix and that BU(t) is a (n × k) matrix with continuous
  functions [0, ∞) →C as entries. Then for any constant (n × k) complex matrix X0, the (n × k) matrix function X(t) = exp(tA)X0 + ∫■t
  0 exp((t −s)A)BU(s) ds is the unique solution of the matrix differential equation dX dt = AX + BU that has the initial value X(0) =
  X0.

  Frame 69: 67 Proof by checking
  What the slide says: so again by frame 38 X(t) −exp(tA)X0 = ∫■t 0 exp((t −w)A)BU(w)dw = exp(tA) ∫■t 0 exp(−wA)BU(w)dw. • To
  check the solution works, let t = 0 to get X(0) = X0. Then we apply standard results of calculus, and work on one entry of the
  matrix at a time. So by the fundamental theorem of calculus, X is a differentiable function of t, with derivative d dt X = A exp(tA)X0
  + exp(0)BU(t) + A exp(tA) ∫■t 0 exp(−wA)BU(w) dw = AX + BU.

  How to study it: Proof checkpoint: identify the assumptions, the statement to establish, and the exact justification for each algebraic
  or analytic step. Reproduce the proof without referring to the slide.


  Frame 70: 68 Solving MIMO
  What the slide says: Corollary For any initial condition X0 and any continuous input U, the solution of (A,B,C,D) is Y (t) = C
  exp(tA)X0 + ∫■t 0 C exp((t −v)A)BU(v) dv + DU(t). Proof. From the theorem 65, we take X(t) = exp(tA)X0 + ∫■t 0 exp((t
  −v)A)BU(v) dv and then Y is uniquely determined by X and U via Y (t) = CX(t) + DU(t).

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 71: 69 Terminology concerning solutions
  What the slide says: The terminology of MATH102/318 Differential Equations reappears. Consider the inhomogeneous
  differential equation dX dt = AX + BU(t). i) The expression X(t) = exp(tA)X0 with X0 arbitrary is known as the complementary
  function, since it satisfies the homogeneous differential equation dX dt = AX. ii) The term ∫■t 0 exp((t −v)A)BU(v) dv is a particular
  integral of the inhomogeneous differential equation. iii) The general solution of the inhomogeneous equation is a particular
  integral plus the complementary function.

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 72: 70 Rational functions
  What the slide says: Let s be an algebraic indeterminate (variable), let g(s) and h(s) be complex polynomials, with h(s) not the
  zero polynomial. Then f (s) = g(s) h(s) is said to be a rational function. The set of all complex rational functions in s is denoted
  C(s), with the usual operations of multiplication, addition, division and differentiation. (i) If the degree of g(s) is less than or equal
  to the degree of h(s), then f (s) is said to be proper rational. If the degree of g(s) is strictly less than the degree of h(s), then f (s) is
  said to be strictly proper. (ii) Suppose that g(s) and h(s) have no common factors other than constants. Then zeros of g(s) give
  zeros of f (s); while zeros of h(s) give poles of f (s).

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.


  Frame 73: 71 The transfer function of (A, B, C, D) (see also 114)
  What the slide says: Definition (Transfer function) The transfer function of (A, B, C, D) is T(s) = D + C(sI −A)−1B. Lemma The
  transfer function may be found by exact arithmetic over C(s) by elementary row operation. Proof. If sI −A is invertible, then the
  following block matrices are row equivalent over C(s): [■A −sI B C D ]■ ∼= [■I (A −sI)−1B 0 T(s) ]■ . We observe that T(s) can be




                                                                      30
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                                      Revision Guide




  computed exactly by matrix multiplication and elementary row operations, which involve rational arithmetic.

  How to study it: System checkpoint: locate u, x, y and any A,B,C,D matrices. Confirm dimensions, distinguish initial-condition effects
  from input effects, and calculate a small special case.


  Frame 74: 72 The transfer function of SISO (A, B, C, D)
  What the slide says: Proposition The transfer function T(s) = D + C(sI −A)−1B of a SISO system is a proper rational function,
  and all the poles are eigenvalues of A. Proof. The characteristic polynomial det(sI −A) has degree n, and leading term sn. A
  cofactor of sI −A is the determinant of a (n −1) × (n −1) submatrix of sI −A and hence is a polynomial of degree less than or equal
  to n −1. Now (sI −A)−1 = det(sI −A)−1adj(sI −A) where adj(sI −A) is the transpose of the matrix of cofactors. Hence the entries of
  (sI −A)−1 are strictly proper rational functions. The eigenvalues of A are precisely the zeros of det(sI −A), hence are the only
  possible poles of entries of (sI −A)−1. Since C, B and D are constant matrices, they do not introduce any more factors involving s,
  so T(s) is a proper rational function.

  How to study it: System checkpoint: locate u, x, y and any A,B,C,D matrices. Confirm dimensions, distinguish initial-condition effects
  from input effects, and calculate a small special case.


  Frame 75: 73 The transfer function of MIMO (A, B, C, D)
  What the slide says: Corollary The transfer function of a MIMO is a (m × k) matrix of proper rational functions, and all the poles
  are eigenvalues of A. This follows from the proof of the Proposition 72. Next we consider the converse of the Proposition.
  Realization means devising a linear system with a given transfer function. [see video: V8 Realizing proper rational functions]

  How to study it: System checkpoint: locate u, x, y and any A,B,C,D matrices. Confirm dimensions, distinguish initial-condition effects
  from input effects, and calculate a small special case.

  . . −αn−1 ■ ■■■■■■■■ , B = ■ ■■■■■■ 0 0 ... 0 1 ■ ■■■■■■ , C = [■ γ0 γ1 . . . γn−1 ]■ , D = 0.

  Frame 77: 75 Proof of realization
  What the slide says: We require to prove T(s) = C(sI −A)−1B. Recall that (sI −A)−1 = det(sI −A)−1adj(sI −A) where the adjugate

  −A)B equals the last column of adj(sI −A), so by transposition, adj(sI −A)B equals the final row of the matrix of cofactors of sI −A,
  where sI −A = ■ ■■■■■■■■ s −1 0 . . . 0 0 s −1 ... 0 ... ... ... ... ... ... ... ... s −1 α0 α1 . . . αn−2 s + αn−1 ■ ■■■■■■■■ We
  compute these one after another, and find that the αj do not appear in these cofactors.

  Frame 85: 83 Realising MIMO as a feedback system
  What the slide says: Differentiators are sometimes regarded as bad components to have in a linear system since they can
  introduce noise. Integrators are preferable, since they depend upon the long term history of system. So it is advantageous to
  produce linear systems without differentiators. Proposition The MIMO system (A, B, C, D) can be realized as a feedback system
  involving taps, matrix amplifiers, summing junctions and integrators, but no differentiators. Proof. We write d dt X = AX + BU in
  the form X = A ∫■ X + B ∫■ U realize as a feedback system with ∫■ and matrix amplifiers A and B.

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.

  Frame 88: 86 Chapter 3 Laplace transforms
  What the slide says: [See video: V11 Laplace transform introduction] Definition (Laplace transform) (i) Suppose that f : (0, ∞)
  →C is a continuous function such that (E) |f (x)| ≤Meβx (x > 0) for some M > 0 and β ∈R. Here β is called the exponential type or
  growth rate. Then we say that f is of exponential type, or satisfies (E). (ii) We then define the Laplace transform by L(f )(s) = ∫■∞ 0
  f (x)e−sx dx (ℜs > β). Sometimes L(f )(s) is written as ˆf (s). Here x, t are time variables; whereas s is the transform variable.

  How to study it: Calculation checkpoint: state the transform rule and its conditions, keep track of initial values, simplify carefully, then
  verify by inverse transformation or substitution.

  Frame 91: 89 Remarks on some functions
  What the slide says: (i) In the above table xα satisfies (E) for α ≥0; for 0 > α > −1, xα diverges as x →0+, but the Laplace
  transform integral exists as an improper Riemann integral. (ii) The Dirac delta function δb is not actually a function, instead δb is
  the measure that assigns unit mass to the point b ≥0 on the line. So ∫■ f (x)δb(dx) = f (b) for all continuous real functions f . (iii)
  The Heaviside function H(x) = 1, x ≥0; H(x) = 0, x < 0; is a step function with a jump at x = 0, so H(x −b) is a step function with a
  jump at x = b. Hence H(x −b) = ∫■ (−∞,x] δb(dt).

  How to study it: Understanding checkpoint: put the definition or claim into your own words, explain the purpose of every symbol, and
  construct a simple example or counterexample.

  Frame 93: 91 Properties of the Laplace transform
  What the slide says: Proposition. Here (E) refers to some M > 0 and β ∈R. (i) The Laplace transform exists for all s > β, and |L(f
  )(s)| ≤ M s−β. (ii) eaxf (x) also satisfies (E) with transform L(f )(s −a). (iii) The Laplace transform is linear so, that if f , g satisfy (E),
  then for all λ, µ ∈C the function λf + µg also satisfies (E) and L(λf + µg)(s) = λL(f )(s) + µL(g)(s). (iv) xf (x) also satisfies (E) and L(f
  )(s) is differentiable with L(xf (x))(s) = −d ds L(f )(s). (v) If f is continuously differentiable and f ′ satisfies (E), then f also satisfies
  (E) and L(f ′)(s) = sL(f )(s) −f (0).

  How to study it: Calculation checkpoint: state the transform rule and its conditions, keep track of initial values, simplify carefully, then
  verify by inverse transformation or substitution.

  Frame 102: 100 Laplace convolution




                                                                     34
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                                  Revision Guide




  What the slide says: [See video: V16 Laplace convolution] Definition. Suppose that f and g both satisfy (E). Then their Laplace
  convolution is f ∗g(x) = ∫■x 0 f (x −y)g(y) dy. Proposition The Laplace convolution is: (i) commutative, so f ∗g = g ∗f ; (ii) linear, so
  (λf + µg) ∗h = λf ∗h + µg ∗h; (iii) multiplicative with respect to the Laplace transform, so f ∗g satisfies (E) and L(f ∗g)(s) = L(f
  )(s)L(g)(s) (s > s0); (iv) associative, so f ∗(g ∗h) = (f ∗g) ∗h.

  How to study it: Calculation checkpoint: state the transform rule and its conditions, keep track of initial values, simplify carefully, then
  verify by inverse transformation or substitution.

  )(s)L(g)(s). The Laplace transform converts convolution to multiplication.

  Frame 106: 104 Lerch’s Uniqueness Theorem
  What the slide says: Theorem Suppose that f and g satisfy (E) and that there exists s0 such that L(f )(s) = L(g)(s) (s > s0). Then
  f (x) = g(x) for all x > 0. Here the Laplace transforms of f and g are equal on the line segment (s0, ∞) of infinite length. The proof
  of this is rather difficult, hence is omitted. There is an inversion formula, credited to Bromwich, which recovers f from its Laplace
  transform F via a contour integral; unfortunately, it is rather hard to use. So if one knows that F(λ) occurs as L(f )(λ) for some f ,
  then the best way to find f is by comparing F with known Laplace transforms in tables, then invoking the uniqueness theorem.

  How to study it: Proof checkpoint: identify the assumptions, the statement to establish, and the exact justification for each algebraic
  or analytic step. Reproduce the proof without referring to the slide.


  Frame 107: 105 Solving an integral equation
  What the slide says: Example To solve the integral equation y(t) = 2eat + ∫■t 0 eb(t−u)y(u) du where a, b are constants with a
  ■= b + 1, and y satisfies (E). Solution. Note that the the integral is a convolution of y with ebt, so by Proposition 100(iii), we have
  ˆy(s) = 2 s −a + ˆy(s) s −b ; after a little reduction we obtain ˆy(s) = 2(s −b) (s −a)(s −b −1);

  the uniqueness of Laplace transforms, we have a unique solution y(t) = 2(a −b)eat −2e(b+1)t a −b −1 . [See video: V17 Solving

  Frame 109: 107 Laplace transform of a differential equation
  What the slide says: Proposition Suppose that y(0) = p0, y′(0) = p1, . . . , y(n−1)(0) = pn−1 and y(n)(t) + an−1y(n−1)(t) + · · · +
  a0y = u(t) where the coefficients are complex constants and u satisfies (E). Then there exists a complex polynomial qn−1(s) of

  degree ≤n −1, that depends only upon the aj and pk, such that (sn + an−1sn−1 + · · · + a0)L(y)(s) + qn−1(s) = L(u)(s) where sn +
  an−1sn−1 + · · · + a0 is the characteristic polynomial.

  Frame 110: 108 Proof
  What the slide says: By repeatedly applying Proposition 91, we have L(y′)(s) = sL(y)(s) −y(0) L(y′′)(s) = sL(y′)(s) −y′(0) L(y(n))(s)
  = sL(y(n−1))(s) −y(n−1)(0), so we can substitute backwards and get L(y′′)(s) = s2L(y)(s) −sy(0) −y′(0) L(y′′′)(s) = s3L(y)(s) −s2y(0)
  −sy′(0) −y′′(0) and thus obtain qn−1(s) with coefficients pj = y(j)(0) as in the initial conditions y(0), . . . , y(n−1)(0). The
  characteristic polynomial in frame 107 is the same as we get from frame 34.

  Frame 113: 111 Transfer function of a Laplace transformed system

  Frame 118: 116 Jordan blocks
  What the slide says: A k × k Jordan block with eigenvalue λ ∈C is the matrix Jk(λ) = ■ ■■■■■■■ λ 1 0 . . . 0 0 λ 1 0 . . . ... 0 ...
  ... 0 ... ... 0 ... 1 0 0 . . . 0 λ ■ ■■■■■■■ J1(λ) = [λ], J2(λ) = [■λ 1 0 λ ]■ , J3(λ) = ■ ■ λ 1 0 0 λ 1 0 0 λ ■ ■

  Frame 119: 117 Jordan canonical form (Jordan normal form)
  What the slide says: [See video: V18 Jordan canonical form] i) Let λ be an entry on the leading diagonal of a square matrix A
  such that all the other entries in the same column as λ are 0. Observe if λ is in row j, then the column vector with 1 in row j and 0
  elsewhere is an eigenvector corresponding to eigenvalue λ. Hence λ is an eigenvalue of A. ii) In a Jordan block, all the entries
  down the leading diagonal are equal to some λ, all the entries in the diagonal above the leading diagonal are 1, and all other
  entries are zero. By (i), λ is an eigenvalue of the Jordan block. iii) We put Jordan blocks of various sizes into a block matrix, so
  that the blocks on the block diagonal are Jordan blocks. Then all the entries below the leading diagonal are zero; all the entries in
  the diagonal directly above the leading diagonal are 0 or 1, and all the entries above this diagonal are all zero.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.


  Frame 120: 118 Eigenvalue terminology
  What the slide says: (i) Each eigenvalue λj has algebraic multiplicity nj, where nj is the largest power of (z −λj) that divides the
  characteristic polynomial of A. (ii) For each eigenvalue λj, there is an eigenvector vj. Let E(λj) = {v : Av = λjv} be the eigenspace.
  The geometric multiplicity of λj is the dimension of E(λj). (iii) For each λj, the geometric multiplicity is the number of Jordan blocks
  that involve λj, so the geometric multiplicity is less than or equal to the algebraic multiplicity. (iv) When a Jordan block has shape
  k × k, where k > 1, it has both eigenvectors and generalised eigenvectors. A generalised eigenvector is v ■= 0 such that (λjI
  −A)mv = 0 for some m = 2, . . . , k.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.


  Frame 121: 119 Example
  What the slide says: Consider the matrix A that is given by A = ■ ■■■■■■■ ■ ■ 2∗ 1 0 0 2 1 0 0 2 ■ ■ 03×1 03×2 01×3 [2∗]
  01×2 02×3 02×1 [■3∗ 1 0 3 ]■ ■ ■■■■■■■ where the eigenvalues are marked 2∗and 3∗; these are the only entries in their
  own column; the eigenspaces are E(2) of dimension 2 since there are two blocks involving eigenvalue 2, and E(3) of dimension 1
  since there is one block involving eigenvalue 3. The eigenvalues are 2, 2, 3, listed according to multiplicity.

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 122: 120 Jordan form of the example
  What the slide says: and the corresponding eigenvectors are e1, e4, e5.The matrix A has Jordan blocks J2(3) ⊕J2(1) ⊕J2(3)
  and A has shape 6 × 6 since 3 + 1 + 2 = 6; the characteristic polynomial is χA(s) = (s −2)4(s −3)2 which is given by the product of
  the diagonal terms of sI −A and has roots 2, 2, 2, 2, 3, 3; the minimal polynomial is m(s) = (s −2)3(s −3)2 since the largest block
  involving eigenvalue 2 is J3(2) and the largest with eigenvalue 3 is J2(3).

  How to study it: Application checkpoint: write the variables and assumptions, derive the governing equation, verify signs, and check
  whether the conclusion makes physical or mathematical sense.


  Frame 123: 121 Computing eigenvalues




                                                                     37
MATH6342 | Linear Systems                                                                                                       Revision Guide




  What the slide says: (i) One way to compute eigenvalues is to solve the characteristic equation, either algebraically or
  numerically. Computers can employ some special algorithms to find approximate values for eigenvalues. (ii) For complex
  matrices up to and including size 4 × 4, it is possible to compute the Jordan canonical form algebraically. However, computing
  JCF by hand is a bore. (iii) In any method, multiple eigenvalues are a technical challenge. MATLAB has a command jordan for
  finding the JCF. (iv) However, computers find it easy to check whether matrices are positive definite, so researchers have found
  clever ways to use linear matrix inequalities instead of computing eigenvalues, as in frame 210.

  How to study it: Matrix checkpoint: identify dimensions, relevant eigenvalues or norms, carry out the algebra, and verify by
  multiplication or substitution.

  and either r(s) = 0 or the degree of r(s) is strictly less than the degree of h(s); ...

  Frame 132: 130 Partial fractions and Laplace transforms
  What the slide says: f (s) = q(s) + r(s) h(s) where r(s)/h(s) is strictly proper. Now by the fundamental theorem of algebra, h(s) = b
  n ∏■ j=1 (s −λj)mj where the λj ∈C are distinct. Then by the usual partial fractions calculation, we look for coefficients ak such
  that r(s) h(s) = ∑■ k ak,nk (s −λk)nk , with integers 1 ≤nk ≤mk. The poles and coefficients are unique.

  decomposition as F(s) = ∑■ aj (s ...

  Frame 137: 135 Chapter 4: Transfer functions, realization and stability
  What the slide says: Consider a SISO linear system Y = LU where L is a linear operator, and such that all the input U and
  output Y satisfy (E). Let ˆY and ˆU be the Laplace transforms of Y and U. Suppose that T(s) is a function such that ˆY (s) =
  T(s)ˆU(s) (s > β). Then T(s) is called the transfer function of the linear system. The transfer function describes all the important
  dynamics associated with (A, B, C, D); see Theorem 159. Theorem (Realizing a SISO by a rational function) Let T be a complex
  rational function. Then there exists a SISO linear system Σ, possibly with feedback, composed of taps, amplifiers, summing
  junctions, integrators, and differentiators, such that the transfer function of Σ is T.

  How to study it: Control checkpoint: label the signals, state whether this is internal or input-output stability, derive the correct
  closed-loop expression, and check the hypotheses of the stability test.


  Frame 138: 136 Proof of realization
  What the slide says: Let the transfer function be T(s) = p(s)/q(s) where p(s) = ∑■n j=0 ajsj and q(s) = ∑■m k=0 bksk are
  polynomials with bm = 1. Consider the differential equation y = n ∑■ j=0 aj dju dtj which has Laplace transform Y (s) = p(s)U(s).
  Also, we can realize the proper rational function 1/q(s) as the transfer function of a SISO system, as in Proposition 74. By
  combining these in series, we realize a system with transfer function T(s) = p(s)/q(s).

  How to study it: Proof checkpoint: identify the assumptions, the statement to establish, and the exact justification for each algebraic
  or analytic step. Reproduce the proof without referring to the slide.


  Frame 139: 137 Transfer function of a Laplace transformed MIMO
  What the slide says: Definition (Transfer function) Consider a MIMO linear system Y = LU where L is a linear operator, and such
  that all the entries of the (k × 1) input U and (m × 1) output Y satisfy (E), and let the initial conditions be zero. Suppose that T(s) is
  a (m × k) matrix of functions such that ˆY (s) = T(s)ˆU(s) (s > β). Then T(s) is called the transfer function of the linear system.
  Corollary Let T be a matrix of complex rational functions. Then there exists a MIMO linear system Σ, possibly with feedback,

  Frame 140: 138 Frequency Response
  What the slide says: Suppose that we have a SISO with Laplace transform ˆY (s) = T(s)ˆU(s). We change variable to s = iω so
  ˆY (iω) = T(iω)ˆU(iω). (Consider input eiωt, with iω on imaginary axis in s plane. We interpret ω as an angular frequency, and t as
  time.) Definition (i) Let T(s) be a rational function. Then the frequency response function is T(iω) where ω ∈(−∞, ∞). (ii) The
  Nyquist plot of T is the curve {T(iω) : −∞≤ω ≤∞}. Nyquist introduced a plot of the frequency response function T(iω) = Γ(ω)eiϕ(ω),
  from which one can glean a great deal of useful information about the linear system. In the MATH6342 Projects, it is helpful to
  produce Nyquist plots of all the frequency response functions in use. Given a real rational transfer function tf , MATLAB has a
  special command nyquist for the Nyquist plot.

  How to study it: Graph checkpoint: identify the quantity on each axis, the parameter being varied, and what poles, zeros, magnitude
  or phase tell you. Draw a quick labelled sketch.


  Frame 141: 139 A Nyquist plot
  What the slide says: Figure: Nyquist plot for the transfer function 1/(s2 + s + 6) The arrows give direction of the Nyquist plot. We
  always plot T(iω) as ω increases from −∞to +∞, so iω goes up the imaginary axis.

  How to study it: Graph checkpoint: identify the quantity on each axis, the parameter being varied, and what poles, zeros, magnitude
  or phase tell you. Draw a quick labelled sketch.


  Frame 142: 140 Another Nyquist plot
  What the slide says: Figure: Nyquist plot looping across itself MATLAB gives a red cross at the point −1. We are interested in
  the net number of times that the contour winds round −1; that is, the number of clockwise windings minus the number of
  anti-clockwise windings.

  Frame 143: 141 Geometrical interpretation of phase and gain
