The terminology of MATH102/318 Differential Equations reappears.
Consider the inhomogeneous differential equation
                         dX
                            = AX + BU(t).
                         dt
i) The expression X (t) = exp(tA)X0 with X0 arbitrary is known as the
complementary function, since it satisfies the homogeneous differential
equation
                               dX
                                    = AX .
                                dt
              ∫︁ t
ii) The term 0 exp((t − v)A)BU(v) dv is a particular integral of the
inhomogeneous differential equation.
iii) The general solution of the inhomogeneous equation is a particular
integral plus the complementary function.
70 Rational functions
       MATH6342 | Linear Systems



Let s be an algebraic indeterminate (variable), let g(s) and h(s) be
complex polynomials, with h(s) not the zero polynomial. Then

                                         g(s)
                               f (s) =
                                         h(s)

is said to be a rational function. The set of all complex rational functions
in s is denoted C(s), with the usual operations of multiplication,
addition, division and differentiation.
(i) If the degree of g(s) is less than or equal to the degree of h(s), then
f (s) is said to be proper rational. If the degree of g(s) is strictly less
than the degree of h(s), then f (s) is said to be strictly proper.
(ii) Suppose that g(s) and h(s) have no common factors other than
constants. Then zeros of g(s) give zeros of f (s); while zeros of h(s) give
poles of f (s).
71 The MATH6342
       transfer function   of (A, B, C , D) (see also 114)
                 | Linear Systems



 Definition (Transfer function)
 The transfer function of (A, B, C, D) is T (s) = D + C(sI − A)−1 B.

 Lemma
 The transfer function may be found by exact arithmetic over C(s) by
 elementary row operation.

 Proof. If sI − A is invertible, then the following block matrices are row
 equivalent over C(s):

                      A − sI B ∼ I (A − sI)−1 B
                    [︃            ]︃ [︃                ]︃
                                    =                     .
                        C      D       0       T (s)

 We observe that T (s) can be computed exactly by matrix multiplication
 and elementary row operations, which involve rational arithmetic.
72 The MATH6342
       transfer function   of SISO (A, B, C , D)
                 | Linear Systems


 Proposition
 The transfer function T (s) = D + C(sI − A)−1 B of a SISO system is a
 proper rational function, and all the poles are eigenvalues of A.
 Proof. The characteristic polynomial det(sI − A) has degree n, and
 leading term s n . A cofactor of sI − A is the determinant of a
 (n − 1) × (n − 1) submatrix of sI − A and hence is a polynomial of
 degree less than or equal to n − 1. Now
                  (sI − A)−1 = det(sI − A)−1 adj(sI − A)
 where adj(sI − A) is the transpose of the matrix of cofactors. Hence the
 entries of (sI − A)−1 are strictly proper rational functions. The
 eigenvalues of A are precisely the zeros of det(sI − A), hence are the only
 possible poles of entries of (sI − A)−1 . Since C, B and D are constant
 matrices, they do not introduce any more factors involving s, so T (s) is
 a proper rational function.
73 The MATH6342
       transfer function   of MIMO (A, B, C , D)
                 | Linear Systems




Corollary
The transfer function of a MIMO is a (m × k) matrix of proper rational
functions, and all the poles are eigenvalues of A.

This follows from the proof of the Proposition 72. Next we consider the
converse of the Proposition. Realization means devising a linear system
with a given transfer function. [see video: V8 Realizing proper rational
functions]

83 Realising MIMO
       MATH6342      as aSystems
                | Linear  feedback system

Differentiators are sometimes regarded as bad components to have in a
linear system since they can introduce noise. Integrators are preferable,
since they depend upon the long term history of system. So it is
advantageous to produce linear systems without differentiators.
Proposition
The MIMO system (A, B, C, D) can be realized as a feedback system
involving taps, matrix amplifiers, summing junctions and integrators, but
no differentiators.

86 Chapter 3 Laplace
      MATH6342         transforms
                | Linear Systems


[See video: V11 Laplace transform introduction]
Definition (Laplace transform)
(i) Suppose that f : (0, ∞) → C is a continuous function such that

                   (E )     |f (x)| ≤ Me βx     (x > 0)

for some M > 0 and β ∈ R. Here β is called the exponential type or
growth rate. Then we say that f is of exponential type, or satisfies (E).
(ii) We then define the Laplace transform by
                          ∫︂ ∞
               L(f )(s) =      f (x)e −sx dx   (ℜs > β).
                            0

Sometimes L(f )(s) is written as fˆ(s). Here x, t are time variables;
whereas s is the transform variable.

89 Remarks on some
      MATH6342       functions
               | Linear Systems




(i) In the above table x α satisfies (E) for α ≥ 0; for 0 > α > −1, x α
diverges as x → 0+, but the Laplace transform integral exists as an
improper Riemann integral.
(ii) The Dirac delta function δb is not actually a function, instead δb is
the
∫︁ measure that assigns unit mass to the point b ≥ 0 on the line. So
   f (x)δb (dx) = f (b) for all continuous real functions f .
(iii) The Heaviside function

                            H(x) = 1,    x ≥ 0;

                            H(x) = 0,    x < 0;
is a step function with a jump at x = 0, so∫︁H(x − b) is a step function
with a jump at x = b. Hence H(x − b) = (−∞,x] δb (dt).

 Proposition. Here (E ) refers to some M > 0 and β ∈ R.

Proposition

(i) L(f )(s) defines a holomorphic (complex differentiable) function of s
on the open right half plane {s ∈ C : ℜs > β};
(ii) L(f )(s) → 0 as s → ∞ along (0, ∞).

(ii) This is similar to Proposition 91(i).

100 Laplace convolution
      MATH6342  | Linear Systems


[See video: V16 Laplace convolution] Definition. Suppose that f and g
both satisfy (E ). Then their Laplace convolution is
                                ∫︂ x
                     f ∗ g(x) =      f (x − y)g(y) dy.
                                   0



Proposition
The Laplace convolution is:
(i) commutative, so f ∗ g = g ∗ f ;
(ii) linear, so (λf + µg) ∗ h = λf ∗ h + µg ∗ h;
(iii) multiplicative with respect to the Laplace transform, so f ∗ g satisfies
(E) and
                 L(f ∗ g)(s) = L(f )(s)L(g)(s)      (s > s0 );
(iv) associative, so f ∗ (g ∗ h) = (f ∗ g) ∗ h.
101 Bounds on convolution
      MATH6342               formula
               | Linear Systems




(i) Change variable to u = y − x.
(ii) is easy.
(iii) We choose M, β such that

               |f (x)| ≤ Me βx ,   |g(x)| ≤ Me βx       (x > 0);

then
                 |f (x − y)g(y)| ≤ Me β(x−y) Me βy = M 2 e βx
so                    ⃓∫︂ x
                            f (x − y)g(y) dy ⃓ ≤ xM 2 e βx
                                             ⃓
                      ⃓                      ⃓
                      ⃓
                           0
so f ∗ g satisfies (E ).

      MATH6342             formula
                | Linear Systems

                     ∫︂ ∞

                     ∫︂0 ∞        ∫︂ x
                   =       e −sx

                   =       e g(y) dy
                             −sy

                        0                  0
                    = L(f )(s)L(g)(s).

The Laplace transform converts convolution to multiplication.
103 Conclusion
      MATH6342of  proof:Systems
               | Linear   associativity




 (iv) This can be proved in a similar way to (i). Alternatively, one use (iii)
 to compute

                L((f ∗ g) ∗ h)(s) = L(f ∗ g)(s)L(h)(s)
                                   = L(f )(s)L(g)(s)L(h)(s)
                                   = L(f ∗ (g ∗ h))(s)

 and then use uniqueness, as discussed below.
104 Lerch’s Uniqueness
       MATH6342          Theorem
                | Linear Systems



Theorem
Suppose that f and g satisfy (E ) and that there exists s0 such that

                     L(f )(s) = L(g)(s)       (s > s0 ).

Then f (x) = g(x) for all x > 0.

Here the Laplace transforms of f and g are equal on the line segment
(s0 , ∞) of infinite length. The proof of this is rather difficult, hence is
omitted. There is an inversion formula, credited to Bromwich, which
recovers f from its Laplace transform F via a contour integral;
unfortunately, it is rather hard to use. So if one knows that F (λ) occurs
as L(f )(λ) for some f , then the best way to find f is by comparing F
with known Laplace transforms in tables, then invoking the uniqueness
theorem.

      MATH6342  | Linearof a differential equation

 Proposition
 Suppose that y(0) = p0 , y ′ (0) = p1 , . . . , y (n−1) (0) = pn−1 and

                  y (n) (t) + an−1 y (n−1) (t) + · · · + a0 y = u(t)

 where the coefficients are complex constants and u satisfies (E ). Then
 there exists a complex polynomial qn−1 (s) of degree ≤ n − 1, that
 depends only upon the aj and pk , such that

          (s n + an−1 s n−1 + · · · + a0 )L(y)(s) + qn−1 (s) = L(u)(s)

 where s n + an−1 s n−1 + · · · + a0 is the characteristic polynomial.
108 Proof
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems



By repeatedly applying Proposition 91, we have

                        L(y ′ )(s) = sL(y)(s) − y(0)

                       L(y ′′ )(s) = sL(y ′ )(s) − y ′ (0)
                 L(y (n) )(s) = sL(y (n−1) )(s) − y (n−1) (0),
so we can substitute backwards and get

                  L(y ′′ )(s) = s 2 L(y)(s) − sy(0) − y ′ (0)

            L(y ′′′ )(s) = s 3 L(y)(s) − s 2 y(0) − sy ′ (0) − y ′′ (0)
and thus obtain qn−1 (s) with coefficients pj = y (j) (0) as in the initial
conditions y(0), . . . , y (n−1) (0). The characteristic polynomial in frame
107 is the same as we get from frame 34.

              (s 3 + 2s 2 − s + 1)ŷ(s) − 7s 2 − 13s + 4 = û(s).
 Remarks (i) Alternatively, we can represent this as an (A, B, C, D) SISO
 system.
 (ii) We often take s such that ℜs ≥ 0, so s is in the right half plane, and
 denote the points on the imaginary axis by s = iω, where ω ∈ R is the
 angular frequency.
111 Transfer function
       MATH6342        ofSystems
                | Linear  a Laplace transformed system



Definition (Transfer function)
Consider a linear system Y = LU where L is a linear operator, and such
that all the entries of the (k × 1) input U and (m × 1) output Y satisfy
(E ), and let the initial conditions be zero. Suppose that T (s) is a
(m × k) matrix of functions such that

                     Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s)    (s > β).

Then T (s) is called the transfer function of the linear system.
112 Solving MIMO
       MATH6342     by Laplace
                | Linear Systemstransforms




Theorem
Let A, B, C, D be constant matrices, and suppose that the input function
satisfies (E ). Then the output Y of the linear system

                             dX
                                = AX + BU
                             dt
                              Y = CX + DU
with initial condition X (0) = 0 in (E ) exists and is uniquely determined,
and the Laplace transform satisfies

                            Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s)

where the transfer function is T (s) = D + C(sI − A)−1 B.
113 Proof
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems


As in frame 65, the solution is determined by
                           ∫︂ t
                  X (t) =       exp(((t − v)A)BU(v) dv
                             0

which a convolution type integral of functions in (E ), since

                ∥ exp(tA)∥ ≤ M1 e β1 t ,   ∥U(t)∥ ≤ M2 e β2 t

so with M = M1 M2 ∥B∥ and β = max{β1 , β2 }, we have

                    ∥ exp(((t − v)A)BU(v)∥ ≤ Me βt

so
                      ∥X (t)∥ ≤ tMe βt ≤ Me (β+1)t
and so X satisfies (E ) and has a Laplace transform. From the differential
equation, dX /dt also satisfies (E ) and has a Laplace transform, and
likewise Y satisfies (E ).

                    s X̂ (s) − X (0) = AX̂ (s) + B Û(s)
                        Ŷ (s) = C X̂ (s) + D Û(s).

       Ŷ (s) = C(sI − A)−1 B Û(s) + D Û(s) + C(sI − A)−1 X (0).

 A k × k Jordan block with eigenvalue λ ∈ C is the matrix

                              λ 1       0 ... 0
                            ⎡                       ⎤
                            ⎢0 λ        1    0 . . .⎥
                            ⎢ ..       ..   ..
                            ⎢                       ⎥
                   Jk (λ) = ⎢  .  0       .     . 0
                                                    ⎥
                                                    ⎥
                            ⎢ .. . .        . .
                            ⎢                       ⎥
                            ⎣.       . 0        . 1⎦
                                                    ⎥

                              0 0 ... 0           λ

                                            λ 1
                                           [︃   ]︃
                    J1 (λ) = [λ], J2 (λ) =         ,
                                            0 λ
                                     λ 1 0
                                    ⎡       ⎤

                           J3 (λ) = ⎣ 0 λ 1 ⎦
                                      0 0 λ
117 Jordan canonical
       MATH6342       form
                | Linear     (Jordan normal form)
                         Systems



[See video: V18 Jordan canonical form] i) Let λ be an entry on the
leading diagonal of a square matrix A such that all the other entries in
the same column as λ are 0. Observe if λ is in row j, then the column
vector with 1 in row j and 0 elsewhere is an eigenvector corresponding to
eigenvalue λ. Hence λ is an eigenvalue of A.
ii) In a Jordan block, all the entries down the leading diagonal are equal
to some λ, all the entries in the diagonal above the leading diagonal are
1, and all other entries are zero. By (i), λ is an eigenvalue of the Jordan
block.
iii) We put Jordan blocks of various sizes into a block matrix, so that the
blocks on the block diagonal are Jordan blocks. Then all the entries
below the leading diagonal are zero; all the entries in the diagonal
directly above the leading diagonal are 0 or 1, and all the entries above
this diagonal are all zero.
118 Eigenvalue
       MATH6342terminology
                | Linear Systems




(i) Each eigenvalue λj has algebraic multiplicity nj , where nj is the
largest power of (z − λj ) that divides the characteristic polynomial of A.
(ii) For each eigenvalue λj , there is an eigenvector vj . Let
E (λj ) = {v : Av = λj v} be the eigenspace. The geometric multiplicity of
λj is the dimension of E (λj ).
(iii) For each λj , the geometric multiplicity is the number of Jordan
blocks that involve λj , so the geometric multiplicity is less than or equal
to the algebraic multiplicity.
(iv) When a Jordan block has shape k × k, where k > 1, it has both
eigenvectors and generalised eigenvectors. A generalised eigenvector is
v ̸= 0 such that (λj I − A)m v = 0 for some m = 2, . . . , k.

121 Computing
      MATH6342eigenvalues
               | Linear Systems




(i) One way to compute eigenvalues is to solve the characteristic
equation, either algebraically or numerically. Computers can employ some
special algorithms to find approximate values for eigenvalues.
(ii) For complex matrices up to and including size 4 × 4, it is possible to
compute the Jordan canonical form algebraically. However, computing
JCF by hand is a bore.
(iii) In any method, multiple eigenvalues are a technical challenge.
MATLAB has a command jor dan for finding the JCF.
(iv) However, computers find it easy to check whether matrices are
positive definite, so researchers have found clever ways to use linear
matrix inequalities instead of computing eigenvalues, as in frame 210.

Corollary
The Laplace transform gives a bijection between

                  span t n e pt : n = 0, 1, . . . ; ℜp < 0
                      {︁                                   }︁


and the set of strictly proper complex rational functions that have all
their poles in the open left half plane {p : ℜp < 0}.

Proof. The Laplace transform of t n e pt is n!/(s − p)n+1 , which is a
strictly proper rational function that has a pole of order n + 1 at p in the
open left half plane. Conversely, any strictly proper rational function has
the form F (s) = G(s)/H(s), where G(s) and H(s) are complex
polynomials such that deg(G) < deg(H) and the poles of F (s) are at the
zeros of H(s). Then F (s) has a unique partial fractions decomposition as

 Consider a SISO linear system Y = LU where L is a linear operator, and
 such that all the input U and output Y satisfy (E ). Let Ŷ and Û be the
 Laplace transforms of Y and U. Suppose that T (s) is a function such
 that
                       Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s)  (s > β).
 Then T (s) is called the transfer function of the linear system. The
 transfer function describes all the important dynamics associated with
 (A, B, C, D); see Theorem 159.

 Theorem (Realizing a SISO by a rational function)
 Let T be a complex rational function. Then there exists a SISO linear
 system Σ, possibly with feedback, composed of taps, amplifiers, summing
 junctions, integrators, and differentiators, such that the transfer function
 of Σ is T .

137 Transfer function
       MATH6342        ofSystems
                | Linear  a Laplace transformed MIMO

Definition (Transfer function)
Consider a MIMO linear system Y = LU where L is a linear operator,
and such that all the entries of the (k × 1) input U and (m × 1) output
Y satisfy (E ), and let the initial conditions be zero. Suppose that T (s)
is a (m × k) matrix of functions such that

                     Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s)    (s > β).

Then T (s) is called the transfer function of the linear system.

Corollary
Let T be a matrix of complex rational functions. Then there exists a
MIMO linear system Σ, possibly with feedback, composed of taps, matrix
amplifiers, summing junctions, differentiators and integrators such that
the transfer function of Σ is T .
138 Frequency Response
       MATH6342 | Linear Systems



Suppose that we have a SISO with Laplace transform Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s).
We change variable to s = iω so Ŷ (iω) = T (iω)Û(iω). (Consider input
e iωt , with iω on imaginary axis in s plane. We interpret ω as an angular
frequency, and t as time.)
Definition
(i) Let T (s) be a rational function. Then the frequency response
function is T (iω) where ω ∈ (−∞, ∞).
(ii) The Nyquist plot of T is the curve {T (iω) : −∞ ≤ ω ≤ ∞}.

Nyquist introduced a plot of the frequency response function
T (iω) = Γ(ω)e iϕ(ω) , from which one can glean a great deal of useful
information about the linear system. In the MATH6342 Projects, it is
helpful to produce Nyquist plots of all the frequency response functions
in use. Given a real rational transfer function tf , MATLAB has a special
command nyquist for the Nyquist plot.
139 A Nyquist
      MATH6342plot
                | Linear Systems




         Figure: Nyquist plot for the transfer function 1/(s 2 + s + 6)

The arrows give direction of the Nyquist plot. We always plot T (iω) as
ω increases from −∞ to +∞, so iω goes up the imaginary axis.
140 Another Nyquist
      MATH6342       plot
               | Linear Systems




                  Figure: Nyquist plot looping across itself

 MATLAB gives a red cross at the point −1. We are interested in the net
 number of times that the contour winds round −1; that is, the number
  of clockwise windings minus the number of anti-clockwise windings.
141 Geometrical
      MATH6342 |interpretation

Let Γe iϕ = x + iy; then for x > 0, we have a point in the right half-plane
with ϕ = tan−1 (y/x) so −π/2 ≤ ϕ ≤ π/2;    √︁ for y > 0, we have a point in
the upper half-plane with ϕ = cos (x/ x 2 + y 2 ) so 0 < ϕ < π.
                                    −1

In the Nyquist plot, the gain and phase can be found from the polar form
of points on the Nyquist contour:
• Γ(ω) = |T (iω)| is the gain, namely the distance of T (iω) to 0;
• ϕ(ω) = arg T (iω) is the phase, namely the angle between T (iω) and
the positive real axis.
Equivalently
                        log T (iω) = log Γ(ω) + iϕ(ω).
In complex analysis, a contour is a continuous curve γ : [a, b] → C such
that γ(a) = γ(b), and γ is piecewise continuously differentiable. The
image of a contour γ under a function f that is holomorphic on γ is also
a contour. The imaginary axis is not a contour, but we can consider
semicircular contours in the right half-plane of radius r , where r → ∞.
Draw [−ir , ir ] on imaginary axis and join ir to −ir by a semicircle in
142 Nyquist’s
      MATH6342locus
                 | Linear Systems



 Proposition (Nyquist’s locus)
 Let R be a rational function that is proper and has all its poles in the
 open left half plane. Then R(iω) for −∞ ≤ ω ≤ ∞ gives a contour in C
 that starts and ends at some c ∈ C where R(s) → c as s → ∞.

 Proof. Write R(s) = c + p(s)/q(s) where degree of p(s) is strictly less
 than the degree of q(s), where R(s) → c as s → ∞. There are finitely
 many poles, at λ such that q(λ) = 0, and there exists δ > 0 such that
 ℜλ < −δ for all poles λ. Hence for −∞ < ω < ∞, the function R(iω) is
 continuously differentiable and R(iω) → c as ω → ±∞. We can write
 ω = tan t where t ∈ (−π/2, π/2), and R(i tan t) → c as t → (−π/2)+
 and → π/2−, so γ(t) = R(i tan t) is a contour in the sense of complex
 analysis. We need∫︁ to show that the length of the contour is finite, or
 equivalently that −∞ |R ′ (iω)|dω converges.
                     ∞
143 Nyquist’s
      MATH6342contour
                | Linear Systems




We have
                                       n
                                                cj
                       R(s) = c +
                                      ∑︂
                                            (s − λj )mj
                                      j=1

where mj are positive integers and ℜλj < 0, so
                                    n
                                          −cj mj
                       R ′ (s) =
                                   ∑︂

                                   j=1
                                       (s − λj )mj +1

where mj + 1 ≥ 2 for all j, so there exists M such that
|R ′ (iω)| ≤ M/(1 + ω 2 ) for all real ω , hence and the length of the
contour is finite. The contour starts and ends at c, since p(s)/q(s) → 0
as s = iω → ±i∞.
144 Gain and Phase
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems



In engineering, the frequency response is relatively easy to measure. The
polar decomposition of the frequency response function gives the gain Γ
and phase ϕ.
• Gain measures how much the device amplifies a signal. If Γ > 1 (so
log Γ > 0), then the device makes the signal bigger.
• The phase describes the relative position of peaks in the output versus
the input.
Definition (Gain and Phase)
Define the frequency response to be T (iω), and make a polar
decomposition T (iω) = Γ(ω)e iϕ(ω) . Then define the gain (or amplitude
gain) of the system to be Γ(ω) = |T (iω)| at angular frequency ω ∈ R;
define the phase (shift) to be ϕ(ω) = arg T (iω). The Nyquist locus is
the curve {T (iω) : −∞ ≤ ω ≤ ∞} in the complex plane.

               | Linear   phase

                         ay ′′ + by ′ + y = u(t).

147 Bode and phase
      MATH6342       plotSystems

                                            Bode Diagram

                                           Frequency (rad/s)

     Figure: Bode plot and phase for the transfer function 1/(s 2 + s + 6)
148 Scales on the| Linear
      MATH6342     Bode Systems
                          plot and phase plot

The Bode plot is the logarithm of the gain log Γ in decibels versus ω in
radians per second, with ω > 0 plotted on a log scale. Going from left to
right on the Bode plot, ω increases from 0 to ∞, and iω goes up the
imaginary axis. Recall that 360◦ is equivalent to 2π radians, and be
careful with the scales on the plots. The phase (or phase shift) ϕ(ω) is
the change in phase of the signal between input and output at a given
input frequency. Phase ϕ in degrees is plotted against ω in radians per
second, with ω > 0 on a log scale.
i) If ϕ(ω) > 0, there is phase gain, so the output runs ahead of the input.
ii) If ϕ(ω) < 0, there is a phase lag, so the output runs behind the input.
iii) If ϕ = 0, then the input and output are said to be in phase.
iv) If ϕ = π (or 180◦ ), then the input and output are in anti-phase so
input peaks emerge as output troughs. Anti-phase signals are used in
noise-cancelling headphones, where a sound is cancelled by a sound in
anti-phase. Anti-phase is also used in optical lens coatings, to reduce
reflections.
Fourier’s integral | Linear Systems
       MATH6342



 Suppose that we feed inputs e iωk into the system, and obtain outputs
 T (iω)e iωk ; then             ∫︂  ∞
                         F (k) =        T (iω)e iωk dω
                                   −∞

 is Fourier’s integral. This is natural to consider in the following examples:
 (i) in the context of music, where e iωk represents a steady tone at a
 single pitch;
 (ii) for electricity, e iωt represents alternating current, which is used for
 mains electricity;
 (iii) in wave optics, Frieser (of Carl Zeiss) and Duffieux observed that this
 is a useful way of assessing the response of optical instruments
 objectively. Note that we have T (iω) for ω > 0 and ω < 0. This is
 natural in Fourier analysis. In MATLAB, Nyquist uses ω ∈ R, whereas
 the Bode plot only uses ω > 0.

151 Cases of the |damped

Consider 1/(s 2 + βs + γ) with γ > 0; poles at λ± = (1/2)(−β ±        ∆)
where ∆ = β 2 − 4γ. Then

   solutions         ∆<0                   ∆=0            ∆>0
    β>0       decaying oscillations  critically damped  exp decay
    β=0             periodic              constant     hyperbolic
    β<0      unbounded oscillations exponential growth exp growth

The damped oscillator is exponentially stable if and only if β > 0 and
γ > 0. When β = 0 and γ > 0, the oscillator is marginally stable. For
β < 0, the oscillator is unstable.
[See video: V20 Harmonic oscillator stability]
152 Stability cases
       MATH6342  | Linear Systems




 Consider dX /dt = AX with X (0) = X0 . This has solution
 X (t) = exp(tA)X0 , and we distinguish the following cases.
 (i) Exponentially stable: there exist M, δ > 0 such that
 ∥X (t)∥ ≤ Me −δt for all t > 0 and all X0 . This occurs when ℜλj < 0 for
 all eigenvalues λj . This is BIBO stable.
 (ii) Marginally stable: X (t) is bounded for t > 0 for all X0 , which
 occurs when ℜλj < 0, or ℜλj = 0 and the corresponding Jordan blocks
 are all of size 1 × 1. Later we will regard this marginal case as BIBO
 unstable.
 (iii) Unstable: X (t) (t > 0) is unbounded for some X0 , which occurs
 when either ℜλj > 0 for some eigenvalue λj of A, or ℜλj = 0 for some λj
 that has a Jordan block of size ≥ 2. This is also BIBO unstable.
153 BIBO stability
      MATH6342  | Linear Systems




Definition (BIBO)
Let (A, B, C, D) be a linear system

                            dX
                               = AX + Bu
                            dt
                             Y = CX + Du
such that for all bounded inputs u(t) for t ∈ (0, ∞), all outputs y are
bounded for t ∈ (0, ∞). Then we say that the system is bounded-input
bounded-output stable, or BIBO stable.
154 Undamped
      MATH6342harmonic    oscillator
               | Linear Systems


 Example
 The undamped harmonic oscillator

                          d 2x
                               + ν 2 x = U0 cos ωt
                          dt 2
 with U0 real and ω, ν > 0 is marginally stable, but not BIBO stable.

                        dt

       MATH6342harmonic    oscillator: complementary
                | Linear Systems
function


The general solution is given by the complementary function plus a
particular integral. The complementary function arises when the input is
zero. For U = 0 the system oscillates at its natural angular frequency ν
                             d
and the general solution of dt X = AX is
                             [︃         ]︃      [︃          ]︃
                               cos νt               sin νt
                    X = c1                 + c2                ,
                              −ν sin νt            ν cos νt

for constants c1 , c2 . In particular, all these solutions are bounded, so we
have marginal stability.
We use this as the complementary function in the following two
contrasting cases.
156 Marginal stability
      MATH6342  | Linear Systems




• For U ̸= 0 and ω ̸= ν, the input has angular frequency different from
the natural angular frequency, and the solution is the complementary
function plus a particular integral

                                                       U0
               [︃           ]︃      [︃          ]︃         [︃         ]︃
                   cos νt               sin νt                cos ωt
       X = c1                  + c2                + 2                   ;
                  −ν sin νt            ν cos νt     ν − ω 2 −ω sin ωt

here the complementary function oscillates at natural angular frequency
ν; whereas the particular integral oscillates at the input angular
frequency ω. These solutions are all bounded. One can obtain these
particular integrals by W3.2, or by guesswork. For aresonant system with
resonant frequency ν > 0, Γ(ω) → ∞ as ω → ν.
157 Resonance
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems




• Let U0 ̸= 0 and ν = ω, so that the input angular frequency equals the
natural angular frequency. Then the general solution is

                                                   U0       t sin νt
              [︃          ]︃      [︃          ]︃      [︃                 ]︃
                 cos νt               sin νt
       X = c1                + c2                +
                 − sin νt            ν cos νt      2ν sin νt + tν cos νt

where the solution oscillates unboundedly; this effect is called resonance.
The input is bounded whereas the output is unbounded, so the system is
not BIBO stable. The term marginal stability is used to describe the
situation in which the complementary function is bounded, whereas the
particular integral is unbounded for suitably chosen bounded inputs; this
entails that the system is not BIBO stable. [See video: V27 resonance]
Resonance occurs where a system is driven by a bounded input at the
natural frequency of the systems, producing an unbounded output.

Lemma
Suppose that A has (not necessarily distinct) eigenvalues such that
ℜλj < 0 for all j = 1, . . . , n. Then there exists M, δ > 0 such that

                    ∥ exp(tA)∥ ≤ Me −δt        (t ≥ 0).


This follows from Lemma 123. The difference between ℜλ ≤ 0 in
Proposition 57(iii) and ℜλ < 0 in the Lemma 158 is subtle, and
historically important in the theory. Maxwell realized that the stronger
hypothesis of the Lemma 158, requiring strict inequality, is needed to
cover the case of multiple eigenvalues, and deal with resonance.

162 Chapter 5 Feedback

Transfer functions and stability criteria: In this chapter we combine ideas
about transfer functions with the notion of stability, so as to obtain
criteria for stability of a system solely in terms of properties of transfer
functions. The idea is to describe the properties of solutions of the
differential equation, without having to solve the differential equations
explicitly. Thus, we go from differential equations to algebra via the
Laplace transform. When building devices from components, the main
operations on the transfer functions are:
• amplification λf (s)
• addition f (s) + g(s)
• multiplication f (s)g(s)
163 Maxwell’s
      MATH6342problem
                | Linear Systems


[See video: V21 stable polynomials]
Definition (Stable polynomials)
A polynomial h(x) is said to be stable if all of its zeros are in the open
left half plane LHP = {s ∈ C : ℜs < 0}.

Problem
Given a monic polynomial, find necessary and sufficient conditions on the
coefficients for the polynomial to be stable.

Finding the zeros exactly can be very difficult. Practical modern method:
given a monic complex polynomial p(s), there exists a complex matrix A
such that det(sI − A) = p(s). Then one can find the eigenvalues of A
numerically. If all the eigenvalues are comfortably in the open left half
plane, then p(s) is stable. We now give a necessary condition for stability,
which is not sufficient. Routh extended this to a sufficient condition.
164 Necessary
      MATH6342condition   for stability
                | Linear Systems


 [See video: V22 Stablepolynomialsrationals1]
 Proposition
 Suppose that h(s) is a monic real polynomial that is stable. Then all the
 coefficients of h(s) are positive.

 Proof. Here h(s) has real coefficients, so h(λ) = 0 if and only if
 h(λ̄) = 0. Hence the roots of h(s) are either real µj < 0; or pairs of
 conjugate complex roots λk and λ̄k with ℜλk < 0, which combine to
 give real quadratic factors (s − λk )(s − λ̄k ) = s 2 − 2sℜλk + |λk |2 . Hence
 h(s) factorizes as
                          n            m
                h(s) =                   (s 2 − 2sℜλk + |λk |2 ),
                         ∏︂           ∏︂
                            (s − µj )
                         j=1         k=1

 where −µj > 0, −2ℜλk > 0 and |λk |2 > 0; hence all the coefficients that
 we obtain on multiplying out are positive.
165 Stable rational
       MATH6342      functions
                 | Linear Systems
                                S


 [See video: V23 Stable rationals]
 Definition (Stable rational functions)
 Let LHP = {s ∈ C : ℜs < 0} be the open left half plane. A rational
 function f (s) is said to be stable if
 (i) f (s) is proper, and
 (ii) all the poles of f (s) are the open left half plane.
 The space of stable rational functions is denoted S.

 Equivalently, f (s) = g(s)/h(s) is stable if
 (i) degree(g(s)) ≤ degree(h(s)), so f (s) is proper, and
 (ii) all the zeros of h(s) have ℜs < 0, so h(s) is stable.
 So a polynomial h(s) is stable as in 164, if and only if 1/h(s) is a stable
 rational function as in 165.)
166 Differential
       MATH6342rings
                 | Linear Systems




 Proposition
 (i) The set S of stable rational functions forms a differential ring on
 RHP = {s ∈ C : ℜs > 0} , so
 (R) S is a ring under pointwise multiplication and addition, so that
 f (s), g(s) ∈ S and λ, µ ∈ C imply f (s)g(s) ∈ S and λf (s) + µg(s) ∈ S;
 (C) multiplication is commutative f (s)g(s) = g(s)f (s);
 (ID2) f (s)g(s) = 0 for all s ∈ RHP implies f (s) = 0 or g(s) = 0 on
 RHP;
 (Diff) For all f (s) ∈ S the derivative f ′ (s) also belongs to S, and
 satisfies Leibniz’s rule (f (s)g(s))′ = f ′ (s)g(s) + f (s)g ′ (s).
 (ii) However, S is not a field.
167 TheMATH6342
        stable rational  functions form a differential ring
                | Linear Systems




 Proof. (i) (R) Multiplication and addition: Given f1 (s) = g1 (s)/h1 (s)
 and f2 (s) = g2 (s)/h2 (s) with degree(g1 (s)) ≤ degree(h1 (s)) and
 degree(g2 (s)) ≤ degree(h2 (s)) we have

                     f1 (s)f2 (s) = g1 (s)g2 (s)/h1 (s)h2 (s)

 where degree(g1 (s)g2 (s)) ≤ degree(h1 (s)h2 (s)). Also, the zeros of
 h1 (s)h2 (s) are either zeros of h1 (s) or zeros of h2 (s), hence are in LHP.
 By partial fractions, we can write f ∈ S as
                                     ∑︂N
                       f (s) = q +           aj (s − λj )−nj .
                                       j=1

 ℜλj < 0. So we can take linear combinations of such f , and stay in S.
 Also f1 (s) + f2 (s) = (g1 (s)h2 (s) + h1 (s)g2 (s))/h1 (s)h2 (s) ∈ S.
 (C) Commutativity of multiplication follows from the corresponding
 property for polynomials;

170 Stability for systems
       MATH6342             and transfer functions
                  | Linear Systems



 [See video: V25 Notions of stability] For a linear system such as
 (A, B, C, B), we have two notions of stability, one is BIBO stability,
 relating to the solutions of the associated differential equation; the other
 is stability of the transfer function as a rational function. The following
 result resolves these two interpretations, and can often determine
 whether transfer functions are stable by basic algebra.
 Theorem
 Let T be a complex rational function. Then T is stable if and only if T
 is the transfer function of some linear system (A, B, C, D) that is BIBO
 stable.
 Proof. Suppose that the system (A, B, C, D) is BIBO stable with
 transfer function T (s); prove that T (s) is stable as a rational function.
 Recall Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s). Then we can choose a bounded input U = 1
 such that Û(s) = 1/s. But Σ is BIBO stable, so Y is bounded,
171 If (A,B,C,D)
        MATH6342 | is BIBO
                    Linear   stable, then T is stable
                           Systems


 so |Y (t)| ≤ M for some M and all t > 0, so
                                 ⃓∫︂ ∞                 ⃓
                      |Ŷ (s)| = ⃓      e −st Y (t) dt ⃓
                                 ⃓                     ⃓
                                 ∫︂ 0∞
                                                         M
                               ≤       e −tℜs M dt ≤        .
                                   0                     ℜs
 Hence Ŷ (s) is holomorphic on {s : ℜs > 0} and Ŷ (s) → 0 as s → ∞
 along (0, ∞). So T (s) = s Ŷ (s) must be proper rational.
 • Suppose that T has a pole at λ; we prove by contradiction that
 ℜλ < 0. If ℜλ > 0, then T (s)Û(s) = T (s)/s also has a pole at λ. But
 Ŷ (s) cannot have a pole at s = λ by Prop 98.
 • Now suppose that ℜλ = 0, so λ = iν for some real ν. The idea is to
 cause resonance, so we let U(t) = cos νt, which is bounded, and
                                   s       1/2      1/2
                      Û(s) =           =        +
                                s2 + ν2   s − iν   s + iν
172 A stable T is| Linear
       MATH6342    realised  by a BIBO stable (A,B,C,D)
                          Systems




has a pole at iν, and hence Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s) has a double (or triple, ...)
pole at iν. Now consider s with ℜs > 0 and s → iν. Now T (s)Û(s)
diverges like 1/(s − iν)2 or 1/(s − iν)3 etc.; whereas Ŷ (s) can only
diverge like M/ℜs at worst. This contradicts the identity
Ŷ (s) = T (s)Û(s). Hence T has no poles on the imaginary axis. Hence
T (s) has all its poles in LHP, so T is stable.
Conversely, suppose that T is stable. Then by Proposition 74, there
exists a SISO (A, B, C, D) such that the transfer function is T and the
eigenvalues λ of A are the poles of T , hence satisfy ℜλ < 0. Then by
Theorem 159, (A, B, C, D) is BIBO stable.

173 Realization
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems




Theorem
(i) Every monic complex polynomial is the characteristic polynomial of
some complex matrix.
(ii) Every proper complex rational function is the transfer function T (s)
of some SISO system (A, B, C, D).
(iii) Every stable complex rational function is the transfer function of
some BIBO stable system (A, B, C, D).

Realization suggests building a gadget with desired properties. In
contemporary English, argument is often used to mean a dispute; it also
means the summary of a story; in mathematics, the term applies to the
angle in the polar decomposition of a complex number. The latter is
denoted arg, or Arg, especially when the values is taken in (−π, π].
MATLAB uses angle for argument, while engineers often use phase.
174 Nyquist’s
      MATH6342criterion
                 | Linearfor stability of T
                          Systems




 [See video: V29 Nyquist plots in MATLAB]
 Here we focus attention on Nyquist’s criterion for stability, which is the
 starting point for the other application. Nyquist’s criterion tells us how
 close a stable system is from instability through feedback. Consider the
 feedback loop with constant feedback −1, so the transfer function is
 T = R/(1 + R).
 Nyquist’s criterion
 Let R be the transfer function of a plant such that R is stable. Suppose
 that the contour R(iω) (−∞ ≤ ω ≤ ∞) does not pass through or wind
 around −1. Then T = R/(1 + R) is also stable, so the feedback system
 with constant feedback −1 is also stable.
175 Functions
      MATH6342in |Nyquist’s  criterion
                  Linear Systems




 We let c = lims→∞ R(s) where c ̸= −1 by assumption. Hence we can
 write R(s) = c + p(s)/q(s) where p(s) and q(s) are polynomials, and
 degree p(s) is less than the degree of q(s). Then

                  R(s)       c + p(s)/q(s)        cq(s) + p(s)
      T (s) =            =                   =                    .
                1 + R(s)   c + 1 + p(s)/q(s)   (1 + c)q(s) + p(s)
 and the degree of (1 + c)q(s) + p(s) equals the degree of q(s), hence
 T (s) is proper.
 Now we check that the poles of T are all in LHP. First, note that poles
 of R give finite values of T . So the possible poles of T (s) are the zeros
 of 1 + R(s), and these are not cancelled by the zeros of R(s).
176 Argument principle
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems



Next we consider the Nyquist contour of R. Let
• N be the net number of times that the Nyquist contour of R winds
around −1, clockwise [we count clockwise windings since we go up the
imaginary axis then should go clockwise to the complete contour in RHP,
see 166]; let
• Z be the number of zeros of R(s) + 1 in the right half plane;
• P be the number of poles of R(s) + 1 in the right half plane;
Then, by the Argument Principle of complex analysis applied to a
semicircular contour in the right half plane,

                             N = Z − P.

(We have N since we are counting clockwise windings, and s describes
the semicircular contour in the negative sense). Here N = P = 0 by
hypothesis, so Z = 0. Hence T has no poles in the right half plane,
hence T is stable.

         and Right   Semicircles

                    -5                  0                   5




          Figure: Semicircular contours in left and right half planes

 The Nyquist contour goes up the imaginary axis, so we count N as the
               number of clockwise windings round −1.

179 Feedback Control
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems




Vehicles are usually under human control.
Example 1. An airliner starts at rest on the runway and then is
accelerated until it achieves lift-off. To achieve this, the engines are run
almost flat out. Once the aircraft reaches cruising altitude, the pilot will
reduce the amount of fuel going into the engines, and fly at a steady
speed.
Example 2. A car on the motor way is not run flat out. Instead, the
driver regulates the speed by means of the accelerator, so that when the
car is going too fast, say over 70 miles per hour, the driver can reduce
the amount of fuel going into the engine, and thus slow down the car.
Likewise the driver can speed up the car by allowing more fuel to go into
the engine. Regulating speed thus involves the continual attention of the
driver; so can we automate this?
180 Feedback controllers
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems




In 1788, the engineer James Watt produced various controllers
(governors) for steam engines. In 1868, Maxwell presented a general
theory of feedback control. The idea is to feed the output from a
machine through a controller back into the input, and thereby regulate
the output. Consider a plant given by a linear system G (m × k) and a
controller represented by a linear system −K (k × m). The output from
G is fed back into the input. The minus sign indicates that we want
negative feedback (if the engine goes too fast, the controller will slow it
down).
• Controllers generally use negative feedback.
• Usually, plants are described by their Laplace transforms.
• Rational transfer functions and controllers are simplest to deal with.
• Proportional-integral-differentiator controllers PID are widely used.
181 Simple feedback
       MATH6342       loop
                | Linear   diagram
                         Systems




                            -K
                   -Ky
                                     y

      u                                      y
              +              G           .
182 Stability problem
       MATH6342  | Linear Systems



 Definition (Simple Feedback Loop)
 (i) Suppose that G has k inputs and m outputs. We choose K to have m
 inputs and k outputs. Then the simple feedback loop (SFL) has transfer
 function
                           T = (I + GK )−1 G.
 (ii) Suppose that G and K are rational matrix functions. The poles of G
 are called open loop poles; the poles of T are called closed loop poles.

 Problem
 Given a SISO system with rational transfer function G, can we find a
 rational controller −K such that T = (I + GK )−1 G is stable rational?

 When G = p/q is strictly proper and stable, Nyquist’s criterion is useful,
 especially for K = 1, the constant negative feedback loop.
183 PIDMATH6342
        controllers
                | Linear Systems




PID (proportional-integral-derivative) controllers K have the form

                                         b
                           K (s) = a +     + cs
                                         s
where a, b, c are constants, usually real. In 1866, Robert Whitehead, a
naval engineer from Bolton, invented PD controllers for torpedoes, in
which the differentiator moves an errant torpedo abruptly back on track.
In other applications, one may wish to have a milder form of controller,
such as a PI controller, which gently guides the system on course. When
selecting a controller, one needs to ensure that the system is stable, and
also adequately responsive. For instance, the steering of a car should be
stable, so that the car does not drift off the intended route, but the
driver should still be able to change direction of the vehicle.
184 Example of proportional
      MATH6342                feedback
                | Linear Systems


Consider a SISO system with transfer function G(s) = p(s)/q(s) with
p, q complex polynomials. The zeros of p are called open loop zeros,
while the zeros of q are called open loop poles. Then the controller
−K = −k with constant k > 0 gives a proportional negative feedback
loop with
                                               p
                      T = (I + K G)−1 G =          .
                                            kp + q
Example. Let p(s) = s 2 and q(s) = bs + c where b, c > 0. Then G(s)
is not proper; whereas
                                         s2
                          T (s) =
                                  ks 2 + bs + c
is proper and has poles in the open left half plane at
                                    √
                              −b ± b 2 − 4ck
                          s=                     ,
                                      2k
so the controlled system with T is stable.

      MATH6342  | Linear Systems     feedback

Example
Find a feedback controller of the form −a − b/s that stabilises the plant
with transfer function G(s) = s/(s − 1)(s + 3).

The plant has poles at s = 1, −3, hence is unstable. We consider
               G         1               s
                   =           = 2                   .
            1 + GK   (1/G) + K  s + (2 − a)s − b − 3

For stability we require positive coefficients, so 2 − a > 0 and
−b − 3 > 0. In particular, we choose b = −4 and a = 0, and get

                             G        s
                                 =          ,
                          1 + GK   (1 + s)2

which is stable.
186 Euclidean
      MATH6342algorithm   for polynomials
                | Linear Systems



How to find the highest common factor of two nonzero polynomials over
C, and express the highest common factor in terms of the original
polynomials.
• Step 0. Given polynomials P0 and P1 with deg P0 ≥ deg P1 , find by
long division polynomials Q1 and P2 such that P0 = Q1 P1 + P2 , where
either P2 = 0 or deg P1 > deg P2 . In the latter case, we continue.
• Step 1. Find by long division polynomials Q2 and P3 such that
P1 = Q2 P2 + P3 , where either P3 = 0 or deg P2 > deg P3 . In the latter
case, we continue.
The degrees of the polynomials satisfy

               deg P0 ≥ deg P1 > deg P2 > deg P3 > . . . ,

so eventually we must have Pn+1 = 0 for some n. This identifies Pn as
the highest common factor of P0 and P1 .
187 Bezout’s identity
      MATH6342        forSystems
                | Linear   polynomials

• Work back. We write
                          Pn = Pn−2 − Qn−1 Pn−1
and eliminate Pn−1 via
                         Pn−1 = Pn−3 − Qn−2 Pn−2 ;
then use
                         Pn−2 = Pn−4 − Qn−3 Pn−3 ;
to eliminate Pn−2 , and so on until we get back to the start, so
                             Pn = MP0 + NP1
where M and N are polynomials, and Pn is the highest common factor.
MATLAB can find the greatest common divisor (highest common factor)
g for polynomials P and Q with g = MP + NQ via the command
>>[g,M,N]=gcd(P,Q)
188 Highest common
       MATH6342       factor
                | Linear      and common zeros
                         Systems




Let G be a complex rational function. Then we can write G = P/Q for
complex polynomials P and Q. We can find the highest common factor
of P and Q by the Euclidean algorithm. Note that the Euclidean
algorithm can be carried out in exact arithmetic–no need to find roots of
polynomial equations.
Lemma
Nonzero complex polynomials P and Q have no common complex zeros
if and only if there exist complex polynomials M and N such that

                            PM + QN = 1.
189 Zeros of the |highest
      MATH6342             common factor
                   Linear Systems




Proof. If P and Q have a common zero λ, then

                     P(λ)M(λ) + Q(λ)N(λ) = 0,

contrary to the equality. Conversely, suppose that P and Q have no
common zeros and carry out the Euclidean algorithm for P and Q to
obtain complex polynomials M, N, R such that R is the highest common
factor of P and Q and PM + QN = R. If R = r is a nonzero constant,
then we can multiply through by r −1 to obtain PM/r + QN/r = 1, as
required. Otherwise, R is a complex polynomial of positive degree, and
hence by the fundamental theorem of algebra has a complex zero λ. Now
R is a factor of both P and of Q, so s − λ is a factor of both P and Q,
so λ is a common zero of P and Q, contrary to assumption.
190 Matrix completion
      MATH6342 | Linear problem
                        Systems



Let R be a commutative ring with 1. Given n ∈ N, and f1 , . . . , fn ,
consider the n × n matrix

                                 f1 , . . . fn
                              [︃               ]︃
                          F =
                                  ∗ ... ∗

where the unspecified entries are to be chosen.
Problem
When can we choose the unspecified entries so that F has an inverse
with entries in R? That is, when does there exist G ∈ Mn×n (R) such
that GF = In ?
This is a really difficult problem, and depends upon R. We will solve the
case n = 2 for some rings that arise from linear systems.
191 Completing
      MATH6342 integer   matrices
                | Linear Systems


Let R = Z, n = 2 and consider a, b ∈ Z. When can we find c, d ∈ Z
such that
                                     a b
                                  [︃     ]︃
                            F =
                                    c d
has an inverse matrix with entries in Z?
(i) Clearly this is impossible when a, b = 0. The inverse

                                   1       d −b
                                        [︃       ]︃
                          G=
                                ad − bc −c a

exists if and only if ad − bc ̸= 0. However, G might have entries in Q,
rather than Z. We require det G, det F ∈ Z such that

                       det(G) det(F ) = det I2 = 1,

so we need to have det F = ±1, that is ad − bc = ±1. Equivalently, c, d
exist if and only if a, b have highest common factor 1.

Proposition
Given f (s), g(s) ∈ C[s], the following conditions are equivalent:
(i) there exist h(s), k(s) ∈ C[s] such that

                                      f (s) g(s)
                                   [︃            ]︃
                             F =
                                     h(s) k(s)

has an inverse G ∈ M2×2 (C[s]);
(ii) f (s)k(s) − g(s)h(s) = ∆ for some ∆ ∈ C \ {0};
(iii) f (s), g(s) have highest common factor 1;
(iv) f (s) and g(s) have no common zeros.

                      ∆ −h(s) f (s)

we are allowed to divide by the nonzero constant ∆.
(ii) ⇔ (iii) Essentially, this is a definition.
(iii) ⇔ (iv) This is Lemma 188.

 Let P(λ) ∈ C[λ]. Then P(1/(1 + s)) gives a stable rational function in s.
195 Coprime factorisation
      MATH6342              in the stable rational functions
                | Linear Systems



 Definition (Coprime)
 Let P(s), Q(s) ∈ S be non zero. We say that P and Q are coprime if
 there exist X (s) and Y (s) in S such that

                        P(s)X (s) + Q(s)Y (s) = 1.


 Proposition
 Let G(s) be a complex rational function. Then there exist P(s) and Q(s)
 in S such that
                                       P(s)
                              G(s) =
                                       Q(s)
 and P(s) and Q(s) are coprime in S, and which may be found in exact
 arithmetic.
196 Example
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems


[See video: V32 Coprime factorization example] Let
                                      s2 + 5
                           G(s) =              .
                                    s2 − s + 1
Then with s = (1 − λ)/λ, we have
                        (1 − λ)2 + 5λ2        6λ2 − 2λ + 1
          G(s) =                            =              .
                   (1 − λ)2 − λ(1 − λ) + λ2   3λ2 − 3λ + 1
Then by the Euclidean algorithm
     (︂ 24λ − 2 )︂(︂             )︂ (︂ 12λ − 9 )︂(︂
                     3λ2 − 3λ + 1 −                 6λ2 − 2λ + 1 = 1
                                                                )︂
           7                              7
so letting λ = 1/(1 + s), we have PX + QY = 1 with P, Q, X , Y ∈ S
(︂ 22 − 2s )︂(︂  3        3     )︂ (︂ 3 − 9s )︂(︂ 6      2
                                                                   = 1.
                                                                )︂
                     −       +1   −                   −       +1
  7(1 + s) (1 + s)2 1 + s            7(1 + s) (1 + s)2 1 + s
197 Proof
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems


(1) We have shown that C(s) and C(1/(s + 1)) are isomorphic. The
issue is to show that we can choose P(s) and Q(s) coprime in S. Since
G(s) is rational, we can write G(s) = M(s)/N(s) where complex
polynomials M, N have no common zeros. [Otherwise, find their highest
common factor by Euclid’s algorithm and divide through by the hcf]
(2) We introduce a new variable λ = 1/(1 + s) and write
                                        (︂ 1 − λ )︂
                          M̃(λ) = λm M
                                             λ
                                       (︂ 1 − λ )︂
                          Ñ(λ) = λm N
                                             λ
where m is the maximum of the degrees of M and N, so that M̃(λ) and
Ñ(λ) are polynomials. Now M̃(λ) and Ñ(λ) have no common zeros. The
problematic case is λ = 0, but we note that M̃(0) is the mth coefficient
of M, and Ñ(0) is the mth coefficient of N; so either M̃(0) or Ñ(0) is not
zero by the choice of m.
198 Conclusion
      MATH6342of  proofSystems
               | Linear



(3) By the Lemma, we can use Euclid’s algorithm to find complex
polynomials X̃ (λ) and Ỹ (λ) such that

                      M̃(λ)X̃ (λ) + Ñ(λ)Ỹ (λ) = 1.

(4) Finally we convert back to the original variable s = (1 − λ)/λ and
introduce                 (︂ 1 )︂                  (︂ 1 )︂
                P(s) = M̃          ; Q(s) = Ñ             ;
                             1+s                     1+s
                            (︂ 1 )︂              (︂ 1 )︂
                 X (s) = X̃         ; Y (s) = Ỹ         ;
                              1+s                   1+s
so that P(s), Q(s), X (s), Y (s) belong to S. Indeed, they are all proper
and the only poles are at s = −1. Furthermore, P(s) and Q(s) satisfy

                        P(s)X (s) + Q(s)Y (s) = 1
199 Coprime factorisation
      MATH6342              algorithm
                | Linear Systems




and
                         M(s)   M̃(1/(1 + s))   P(s)
                G(s) =        =               =      .
                         N(s)   Ñ(1/(1 + s))   Q(s)
We have shown that C(s) is the field generated by S.
Algorithm
1) Write G(s) as a quotient of polynomials in s with no common zeros.
2) Introduce λ via s = (1 − λ)/λ and clear the denominators.
3) Apply the Euclidean algorithm for polynomials in λ.
4) Convert back to the original variable s by λ = 1/(1 + s).

Proposition

rational K = X /Y is such that

                   G           K          1         GK
                       ,           ,          ,
                1 + GK      1 + GK     1 + GK     1 + GK
are all stable rational functions.

                         G         P/Q

                         K         X /Y
                             =            = X Q;

                    1 + GK   1 + PX /QY
Given any rational SISO, we have produced an algorithm for finding a

204 Internal stability
       MATH6342        of Systems

 Definition (Internal stability)
 The SF L system is internally stable if all the transfer functions in the
 matrix Φ are stable, so
                       1      G     GK      K
                          ,      ,      ,       ,
                    1 + GK 1 + GK 1 + GK 1 + GK
 all belong to S.

 The idea is that we want all the junctions in the diagram to be stable.
 (Think of the x, y, z as representing the temperature of the components
 of a tumble drier; we need to ensure that these do not overheat.)
205 Youla’s stabilising
      MATH6342   | Linearcontrollers
                          Systems


 Consider a SISO system (A, B, C, D) with transfer function G(s), and
 consider the corresponding simple feedback loop with rational controller
 K . We now allow G(s) to be an arbitrary rational function, and write it
 in the form G = P/Q with coprime P, Q ∈ S as in Frame 195.
 Theorem
 Suppose that G = P/Q has a coprime factorisation PX + QY = 1 where
 P, Q, X , Y ∈ S. Then K = X /Y internally stabilises SF L.

 Actually Youla found all of the stabilising controllers, but we give the
 simplest case. By previous results, there exists another SISO such that
 K is the corresponding transfer function, so in this sense we have shown
 that any SISO with rational transfer function can be stabilized by a
 rational (possibly unstable) controller. In applications, one may have
 other criteria in mind when selecting a controller. In the project topics,
 there are discussions of how one can tune controllers so that they ensure
 stability, but allow the system to be responsive.
206 Proof
      MATH6342 | Linear Systems




Suppose that K = X /Y . Then, as in frame 200,

                               X P   Y Q + XP    1
                I + GK = 1 +       =          =    ;
                               YQ       YQ      YQ
     1
so 1+GK = Y Q and hence Φ has all entries in S, since

              1                 Y Q −Y P                      −Y Q
            ⎡                ⎤ ⎡                                   ⎤
                   −P/Q  −1
   Φ = YQ ⎣ X /Y     1  −X /Y = X Q Y Q
                             ⎦ ⎣                              −X Q ⎦ .
           PX /Y Q P/Q    1      XP Y P                       YQ

Hence we have found a controller that internally stabilizes the simple
feedback loop system.
207 Self-adjoint
       MATH6342 matrices

           z = (zj )nj=1 and w = (wj )nj=1 , and introduce the inner product
 We write ∑︁
 ⟨z, w⟩ = nj=1 zj w̄j . We define the adjoint of a n × n matrix A = [ajk ]
 by A′ = [ākj ], interchanging the rows and columns and taking the
 complex conjugate. If A is real then A′ = A⊤ , the transpose.
 Proposition
 Suppose that A is self-adjoint, so A = A′ .
 (i) Then the eigenvalues of A are all real, and
 (ii) there exists a unitary matrix U such that UU ′ = U ′ U = I and
 A = U∆U ′ where ∆ is a real diagonal matrix.

 The eigenvectors Xj of A corresponding to distinct eigenvalues are
 orthogonal, so we can choose them so that ⟨Xj , Xk ⟩ = 0 for j ̸= k and
 ⟨Xj , Xj ⟩ = 1. Then U = [X1 . . . Xn ] satisfies U ′ U = I. Also U ′ AU is a
 diagonal matrix.
208 Positive definite
      MATH6342        matrices
                 | Linear Systems




 An n × n complex matrix K is said to be positive definite if K = K ′ and
 ⟨K z, z⟩ > 0 for all z ∈ Cn such that z ̸= 0. Beware that the product of
 positive definite matrices is generally not positive definite.

 Lemma (MATH220 Theorem 5.15)
 Let K be a (n × n) complex matrix such that K = K ′ . Then the
 following are equivalent:
 (i) ⟨K z, z⟩ > 0 for all z ∈ Cn such that z ̸= 0;
 (ii) the eigenvalues κj of K are all real and κj > 0 for all j;
 (iii) the leading principal minors ∆j of K are all positive, so ∆j > 0 for
 all j.

 For medium sized matrices, computers can handle (i).
209 Principal minors

computer program, a for loop.

>> n = 3 [for example]
>> minors=zeros(1:n) [somewhere to put the result]
>> for k = 1 : n [introduce a running variable k]
>> minors=det(A(1:k,1:k)); [compute the determinant of the top left
k × k corner]
>> end [end the for loop]

Theorem
Suppose that A is a complex n × n matrix and that there exists a positive
definite matrix K such that Q = −(A′ K + K A) is also positive definite.
Then all the solutions of
                              dX
                                  = AX
                               dt
are bounded on (0, ∞).

Note that a positive definite K satisfies K = K ′ and
(A′ K + K A)′ ) = (K A + A′ K ), so the issue is whether Q satisfies the
equivalent conditions (i)-(iii) of Lemma 208. One can either (i) try many
positive definite K , and test whether Q is also positive definite, or (ii)
choose a positive definite Q, and try to find K .
211 Proof of Lyapunov’s
      MATH6342            criterion
                | Linear Systems


 To show that ⟨X (t), X (t)⟩ is bounded, the trick is to consider
 V (t) = ⟨K X (t), X (t)⟩. Observe that V (t) ≥ 0 for all t ≥ 0, and use the
 differential equation to find
                 dV      ⟨︂ dX                  dX ⟩︂
                      = K       , X (t) + K X ,
                                       ⟩︂ ⟨︂
                  dt     ⟨︁ dt                   dt
                      = K AX (t), X (t) + K X (t), AX (t)
                                         ⟩︁ ⟨︁              ⟩︁

                      = K AX (t), X (t) + A′ K X (t), X (t)
                         ⟨︁              ⟩︁ ⟨︁               ⟩︁

                      = − −(A′ K + K A)X (t), X (t)
                            ⟨︁                       ⟩︁

                      = − QX (t), X (t)⟩ ≤ 0.
                            ⟨︁

 Hence V (t) is decreasing on (0, ∞). Since K is positive definite, the
 eigenvalues of K are κ1 ≥ κ2 ≥ · · · ≥ κn , where κn > 0; so by W3.4

          0 ≤ κn ⟨X (t), X (t)⟩ ≤ ⟨K X (t), X (t)⟩ ≤ ⟨K X (0), X (0)⟩,

 and so ∥X (t)∥ ≤ (⟨K X0 , X0 ⟩/κn )1/2 for all t ≥ 0.
212 Sylvester’s
       MATH6342equation
                | Linear Systems




Given n × n complex matrices A, B and C, the problem is to find a n × n
matrix Y such that
                          AY + Y B = −C.


Proposition
There are three possibilities for Sylvester’s equation: either
(i) there exists a unique solution Y for all C;
(ii) there exist infinitely many solutions Y for some C;
(iii) there does not exist any solution for some C.
213 Cases of Sylvester’s
      MATH6342            equation
                | Linear Systems
Proof.

 In terms of the matrix entries, we can write A = [ajk ], B = [bjk ] and
 C = [cjk ], and then the unknown Y = [yjk ] is given by the system
                        n                 n
                             ajℓ yℓk +         yjℓ bℓk = −cjk ,
                       ∑︂                ∑︂

                       ℓ=1               ℓ=1

 which is a linear system of n2 equations in the n2 unknowns [yjk ]. So the
 above possibilities arise from the general theory of linear equations.
 Gauss–Jordan elimination reduces this system to reduced echelon form
 and gives the solutions in cases
 (i) if the rank of the system is n2 and the system is consistent, then there
 exists a unique solution Y for any C, and
 (ii) if the rank of the system is less than n2 and the system is consistent,
 then there are infinitely many solutions Y for some C.
 (iii) If the system is inconsistent, then there is no solution for some C.
 For instance, case (iii) arises when A = B = 0, and C ̸= 0.
214 Sylvester’s
       MATH6342criterion
                | Linear Systems



Remark. Fix A and B. Then the transformation
T : Mn×n (C) → Mn×n (C) : Y ↦→ AY + Y B is linear on the
n2 -dimensional vector space Mn×n (C), so either:
(i) T is invertible, and for all C there exists a unique Y such that
T (Y ) = −C; or
(ii), (iii) T is not invertible and hence does not have full rank, so
T (Y ) = −C has no solution for some C.
Proposition
Given A and B, Sylvester’s equation AY + Y B = −C has a unique
solution Y for all C if and only if A and −B have no common
eigenvalues.

Remark MATLAB regards Sylvester’s equation as AY + Y B + C = 0,
hence gives Y = lyap(A, B, C).

 gives the unique solution to

 Corollary
 Suppose that A is a n × n complex matrix such all its eigenvalues are in
 the open left half plane {λ ∈ C : ℜλ < 0}. Then for all positive definite
 P, the integral            ∫︂    ∞
                       L=             exp(tA)P exp(tA′ )dt
                              0
 gives a positive definite L such that

                               AL + LA′ = −P.

 From the characteristic equation, it follows that λ is an eigenvalue of A,
 if and only if λ̄ is an eigenvalue of A′ . Hence the integrals converge, as in
 frame 216. Note that exp(tA)P exp(tA′ ) is positive definite by exercise
 A3.4. Indeed ⟨exp(tA)P exp(tA′ )Y , Y ⟩ = ⟨P exp(tA′ )Y , exp(tA′ )Y ⟩ is
 positive and continuous for t > 0 and Y ̸= 0.

225 Glossary of linear
       MATH6342         system
                 | Linear       terminology
                          Systems



 (A,B,C,D): standard linear system, usually with constant matrices;
 angular frequency see ω;
 amplitude: height of the crest of signal above the average level,
 regarded as a wave;
 BIBO: bounded input and bounded output stable system;
 Bode plot: plot of log gain and phase against angular frequency ω > 0;
 closed loop: system with feedback loop;
 differentiator: operation d/dt of differentiation with respect to time t;
 frequency domain: linear system in terms of ω, where s = iω;
 frequency response function: transfer function T (s) when s = iω and
 ω ∈ R;
 gain: (or amplitude gain)
                        ∫︁ modulus of the transfer function, denoted Γ;
 integrator: operation of integration with respect to time t, from
 t = 0;
226 Glossary of linear
       MATH6342         system
                 | Linear       terminology continued
                          Systems



JCF: Jordan canonical form of square matrix (equivalently, Jordan
normal form);
L̂: Laplace transform of L;
LHP: Open left half plane {s ∈ C : ℜs < 0};
MIMO: multiple input and multiple output linear system;
Nyquist plot: graph in complex plane of T (iω) for −∞ < ω < ∞;
open loop: system without feedback, (also called straight-through);
phase: (or phase shift) argument of the transfer function, denoted ϕ;
sometimes expressed in degrees
resolvent: of square matrix A is (sI − A)−1 ;
RHP: open right half-plane {s ∈ C : ℜs > 0};
s-domain: linear system in terms of Laplace transform variable s;
SFL: simple feedback loop system;
SISO: single input and single output linear system;
227 Glossary of linear
       MATH6342         system
                 | Linear       terminology, concluded
                          Systems




state-space model: linear system in terms of functions of time t, as in
ss(A, B, C, D);
summing junction: operator ⊕ for adding signals;
tap: operator ⊙ for making a signal go along two routes;
t: time, with t ∈ (0, ∞);
transfer function: T (s) multiplies the Laplace transform of the input to
give the Laplace transform of the output;
unit impulse function: δ0 the unit point mass at time t = 0, also
known as Dirac delta function.
omega ω: angular frequency, often abbreviated to ‘frequency’, usually
real-valued; usually expressed as radians per second.
